| Bilgewater → Noxian capital, direct | **33 days** | **28–38 days** | Authorial working estimate only; no verified direct duration. It is not “18 + 19,” and it is not a newly surveyed route. |
| Noxian capital → Bilgewater, direct | **33 days** | **28–38 days** | Separate direct route, same production default until a specific scene supplies a cause for variation. |
| Piltover ↔ Zaun, ordinary chosen urban journey | Scene-specific hours | No universal citywide figure | Record the actual level, access and checkpoints. A raid or broken lift may matter more than straight-line distance. |

Windows are allowances, not guaranteed maximums. Add **3–10 days** when a specific documented delay is actually used: missed sailing, inspection, ship repair, weather or blocked access. Never quietly shrink a journey to rescue an episode collision. A faster method uses its already-owned capacity, availability, access and limitations; do not make an earned portal ability undergo a new acquisition gate.

### Bilgewater foothold worked example

This is a workable production sequence, not a shipment event already accepted:

1. D14: a local intermediary agreement is made, establishing who can actually receive a shipment.
2. D21: first dispatch leaves Piltover/Zaun.
3. D43: first delivery reaches Bilgewater at the default 22-day allowance; port arrival alone does not establish a foothold.
4. D45: a local commercial result is observed; a report/reply departs.
5. D67: the reply reaches Zaun. Heisenberg can now react to the result rather than know it on D45.
6. D70: a follow-up dispatch leaves; D92 arrival supports repeatable access and local consequences before D120.

The XIV.3/D84 card can show the already-earned foreign result through an accurately dated Bilgewater scene, a returned representative or the D67 report. Keep narrator/audience knowledge separate from Walter's knowledge. Neither dispatch creates authority over the harbor or invents a champion patron.

## 4. Season 3 common clock

Use departure **D120**, chosen passage **28 days**, capital arrival **D148**. The gang's first travel choice is a journey it elects to take, not proof it lacks spatial abilities. Before drafting the departure, use any actually established destination reference; if the gang chooses a valid faster method, update D148 and the newcomer-residence offset together, while keeping the relative R-day plot calendar. Define **R0 = D148**, the gang's Season 3 Noxian arrival anchor. The Deadpool narration hijack can lead into this arrival while briefly recounting earlier Elsewhered time; it does not move his arrival to R0.

Selected newcomers' residence by R0 is **Δ + 148 days**, allowing individual small arrival offsets because “around the opening” is not one exact instant. This gives a concrete future minimum runway without asserting the unknown Δ. Their source-snapshot memories remain fixed; local experiences are newly planned events, not later home-universe DLC/film/comic memories.

For each newcomer, separate:

- **E0–D0:** earlier interval not numerically reconstructed. Specify only a small plausible set of necessary local experiences in the offscreen ledger; no unexplained mature empire, universal map mastery or all-cast acquaintance.
- **D0–D49:** survival/contacts and first consequential local bargain or refusal.
- **D50–D102:** changed relationship, cost, reputation or resource resulting from that choice.
- **D103–D148:** concrete position/injury/custody/knowledge carried into the Sins' arrival.

Those bins are opportunities for earned offscreen history, not orders that every character must reach the same level of assimilation. Loyalty to Odin, reluctance to serve, desire to return home and individual volatility still matter. A powerful outsider can remain isolated for months.

### Matt's portal-aware three-block case structure

The efficient-kit correction applies to travel as well as fights. Kaze's known/saved endpoint rules remain live. After Noxian arrival he establishes an endpoint at the gang's actual lodging and retains the legitimately known Zaun endpoint. Matt negotiates two specified passages; Kaze agrees because the case bears on Heisenberg and the gang's existing enemies. This buys neither Matt's allegiance nor permanent transport service.

| Matt block | R-days | Concrete work and change |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Noxus I | R0–14 | Obtain the courier's limited account and the product/works connection; Talon's recovery removes the easy local route. Jesse decides what information to entrust. |
| Assisted transfer | R15 | On-page agreed portal passage between actual valid endpoints. No invented month at sea. Establish the R54 return rendezvous while they can communicate. |
| Zaun | R15–53 | Challenge the false voluntary-transfer account; physically intercept an already-scheduled delivery that corroborates the route; protect the foreman's coworkers and recover earned pay so the foreman can choose to leave. These are active actions and opposition, not an immovable thirty-nine-day queue. |
| Assisted return | R54 | Kaze opens the agreed route; Matt returns with the consenting source and actual findings. If the case resolves sooner and a valid earlier passage is arranged, move this date forward rather than force waiting. |
| Noxus II | R54–126 | Protect the source against Shang's false account, reconcile Jesse's local observation, contest the delivery route and then meet the Katarina/Talon collision at R82. Arrival does not make hostile people agree or grant instant public proof. |

A portal solves distance. It does not compel a witness, supply a missing fact or communicate a change of rendezvous without a channel. Do not invent missing channels when a built Chris/Teemo/Arthur link actually supplies the information to an authorized participant.

### Season 3 act allocation on the corrected calendar

| Act | R-day span | Matt's position / causal state |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| I | R0–10 | Noxus, tracing the initial local link |
| II | R11–24 | Noxus through R14; portal to Zaun R15; active case begins |
| III | R25–38 | Zaun, exposing the false transfer story and opposing its enforcers |
| IV | R39–52 | Zaun, delivery corroboration and the source's practical freedom to leave |
| V | R53–66 | Return by portal R54; source protection and Noxian corroboration |
| VI | R67–80 | Noxus; Jesse and Matt counter the local discrediting attempt |
| VII | R81–98 | R82 Katarina/Talon collision changes what the evidence can accomplish |
| VIII | R99–126 | Noxus; case feeds the final chosen operation and its human consequences |

Swain/Trifarix are the established governmental context. Riven's coerced arrival and Briar's escape occur as new on-page branch events in their assigned cards. Publication order does not place them automatically.

## 5. Communications and knowledge ledger

- **Ordinary carried letters/couriers:** use the relevant route allowance, plus actual dispatch wait if no vessel is departing. A letter can leave earlier than a later passenger; it does not move faster merely because its information is important.
- **Local city messages:** normally same scene/day only when the runner, contact point and path exist; patrols or detention can delay them.
- **Scheduled return reports:** departure date, custody, arrival date, who reads it, and what it actually says must be recorded. Lost or intercepted reports affect only actors who depended on them; narrator knowledge is separate.
- **Swain's ravens:** a legitimate observation is not a global transmission network for other characters. Use established source-specific perception only at the observed moment; distance/range, report scope or an unestablished remote channel cannot be invented from the calendar. The Sins do not borrow his network by proximity.
- **Black Rose communications:** record the specific established contact/channel and its access. “They use magic” is not permission for every member to share all knowledge instantly.
- **47's compatible-device interface:** local compatibility does not create intercontinental internet. Chris's engineering does not automatically supply unselected long-range radios or satellites.
- **Deadpool narration:** his brief audience recap may explain only his actual lived Noxian activities and relevant jokes; it is not a letter to other actors or omniscient recap of every Elsewhered character.

Minimal ledger fields for every consequential interregional move/message: **actor/object; departure day/place; chosen method; ordinary allowance; known delay; arrival day/place; what changes hands; who learns what and when**.


# Season 3 — operative ensemble and episode sequence

## The actual season problem

The Sins arrive in Noxus looking for trouble worth their time. They choose to confront a privately backed fighting company whose veterans profit from the intimidation and equipment trade surrounding Ambessa's expedition. The gang takes the company's valuable stock and defies its claim to the district. Their reward becomes a place to stay, money, attention, and eventually work they choose.

What they have damaged is one part of a private procurement scheme: patrons turn public fighting reputations, indebted labor, imported enhancement products and restricted research into profitable military usefulness. The scheme has cooperating parties with conflicting goals, not a single mastermind who arranged the whole season. One recurring outer-city works compound, its approach road, nearby inhabited streets and associated fighting venue make the conflict legible. These are draft unnamed local settings, not invented official atlas locations.

The immediate Season 3 spine is whether the Sins can keep their people, self-chosen work and new stake without becoming somebody else's enforcement arm. The particular procurement scheme can be dismantled or reorganized by the conclusion without overthrowing Noxus, ending the Black Rose, or eliminating Heisenberg's empire.

The four connected conflicts are public value, ownership of useful people, conditional belonging, and cross-border criminal trade. An event must move at least two of them. Nobody must join a single crossover team for this to work.

## Working allocation and calendar

Use eight three-episode acts, twenty-four working cards, as the committed working capacity estimate. This is more credible for the selected ensemble than forcing twenty-plus principal figures into nine episodes. The working allocation may change if scene length requires it; twenty-four is not represented as a previous user selection. Most episodes stay with the Sins' immediate problem, with one consequential secondary strand and brief bridges. Supporting characters do not each receive an origin episode.

R0 = the gang's arrival at the Noxian capital after the separately planned Season 2 departure and journey. Suggested act windows: I R0–10; II R11–24; III R25–38; IV R39–52; V R53–66; VI R67–80; VII R81–98; VIII R99–126. Days inside a card remain adjustable. Explicit cuts absorb treatment, repair, ordinary earning, information gathering and travel without asserting every episode is one day.

Matt: Noxus R0–14; negotiated Kaze-assisted passage to a genuinely known Zaun endpoint at R15; active Zaun case work R15–53; a second agreed passage back at R54; Noxus thereafter. These are two consensual uses of an existing ability, not Matt acquiring it or Kaze becoming his permanent chauffeur. Communication waits for the selected carrier/ship unless an established communication mechanism exists. 47 stays in the Zaun strand for this season's core design; a physical Noxus visit is unnecessary. Foreign consequences can precede Heisenberg's personal presence.

The newcomers arrived near the opening of Season 2, so their unseen interval predates R0. Do not silently equate that interval to seventeen days. Early adaptation below fits the first several weeks; consolidation occupies whatever further weeks the shared calendar actually provides. The elapsed total remains the shared ledger's responsibility.

## Ten newcomer tracks: exact footholds, actions, reversals and required payoffs

| Character | Initial offscreen foothold and objective | First consequential choice on-page | Reversal and final required contribution |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Thor | First weeks: establish his immediate surroundings and search for news of a way home; accepts limited practical help, not Noxian nationality. Later: a place to sleep and a reputation for reacting badly to coercion. No established portal or Odin contact. | Pursues a patron's claimed access to travel knowledge; agrees only to one bounded service, not enlistment. | The patron can sincerely offer access while overstating what the knowledge can do. Thor refuses to let that uncertainty turn him into permanent property. His later interruption of a deployment changes a physical route; it does not solve every plot by strength. |
| Heimdall | First weeks: question useful locals and seek a route to Odin. Later: obtains entry to a restricted information exchange by making his perception valuable. No assumption that Thor and Heimdall arrived together. | Accepts access in return for a specific judgment about a negotiation, maintaining contempt for the patron. | He correctly reads an immediate intention yet draws a bad larger conclusion from incomplete facts. He later refuses an order presented as the price of access. This exposes the patron's political position without pretending intent-reading supplies world knowledge. |
| Deadpool | First weeks: food, lodging and small paid fights. Later: a mixed reputation and offended clients because he walked away from a job once its human stakes became clear. He has not secretly run the Sins' Season 2. | Briefly hijacks narration; his self-serving account collides with a visible consequence when an aggrieved local recognizes him near the gang. | He initially tries to turn the Sins' publicity into cover for his own problem; admitting his actual part provides a needed witness and specific location. Later he stays for the difficult portion of a chosen operation instead of merely delivering a joke. Not a new permanent Sin. |
| Tai Lung | First weeks: survive and test what prowess earns here. Later: win genuine recognition in organized fighting, with a room and material reward rather than a whole faction. The Dragon Scroll grievance remains unfinished. | Challenges the public claim that the Sins' disruption eclipses disciplined martial greatness. This need not begin as a lethal duel with Kaze. | A patron treats him as an interchangeable attraction and requests service inconsistent with the respect promised. His consequential choice denies the scheme a public demonstration and makes its advertising fail. Recognition does not instantly heal his grievance or make him the gang's friend. |
| Maul | First weeks: confirm displacement and attempt to locate a route home. Later: acquire a small, earned contact chain by trading work or leverage; no transported syndicate. | Interferes with the same private freight route the Sins are attacking because it carries access and information he wants. | He tries to convert mutual enemies into dependent allies. Others refuse the terms. His intervention opens one opportunity and creates another hazard; the late payoff denies him control of the people he hoped to recruit, without generic redemption or automatic alliance with Starkiller. |
| Shang Tsung | First weeks: exploit his salesmanship for shelter and customers; cautiously learn local terms. Later: sells limited, demonstrated services and makes exaggerated claims to gain access to research patrons. No transported Well of Souls. | Offers an apparently useful solution to a patron's problem exposed by the Sins' success. He competes for patronage rather than already controlling local science. | Proof of one useful service does not validate his grander claims. Jesse/Matt connect a concrete discrepancy to a real affected person; this causes a client to withdraw a resource he needs. His response reveals opportunism instead of importing Titan powers. |
| Riddick | First weeks: learn enough terrain and supply to remain independent. Later: repeated work and pursuit establish a limited local reputation, not an army or civic leadership. | Takes possession of goods or passage the private procurement house considers its own and refuses to surrender them, crossing the Sins' pursuit. | His route becomes unsafe after the Sins' public escalation. He bargains for a specific shared exit while keeping his own objective. The payoff is a physically earned path through the works/outer district, not automatic knowledge of every tunnel. |
| Dutch and established group | Twenty-two newcomers plus the already present Arthur make the approved twenty-three-name roster: Dutch, Hosea, Arthur, John, Abigail, Bill, Javier, Micah, Sadie, Charles, Lenny, Sean, Kieran, Susan, Karen, Mary-Beth, Tilly, Uncle, Swanson, Pearson, Strauss, Molly and Trelawny. No duplicate Arthur or imported Jack. Early weeks: supply and shelter. Later: a modest camp and separate work arrangements, not instant collective enlistment. | Dutch welcomes Arthur while offering the Sins a common opportunity; different camp members have different concerns. A successful joint action supplies the camp and increases a patron's leverage. | Dutch frames a more restrictive bargain as the cost of freedom. Arthur refuses a claim on his new family without pretending he never cared about the old one. Camp members make materially distinct choices; the final required result is that Dutch cannot promise their unanimity or the Sins' service. No late-game betrayals are backdated. |
| Magneto | First weeks: distance from local causes while seeking a tolerable place to exist. Later: an independent residence and limited relationships earned over time. Use selected disappointment and resolve, not a reset conqueror. | Refuses a local demand that acceptance depend on compulsory usefulness; intervenes over one concrete person or place, not a declaration that Runeterran mages are mutants. | His intervention draws dependents and political claims he did not invite. He chooses a bounded commitment that denies a patron ownership of those people without solving all Noxian inequality. No exceptional later feats are needed. |
| Lord Starkiller | Immediate post-dark-ending reconstruction snapshot. First weeks: assess unfamiliar environment and respond to his learned expectations of coercion. Later: secure shelter and limited equipment attention by negotiated work; no new medical dependency invented. | Violently rejects a specific attempt to direct him as property, drawing the notice of competing recruiters. The force of the refusal does not automatically establish freedom from his own conditioning. | An offer of autonomy conceals terms of service; he makes one observable choice to refuse the term, not an instantaneous heroic conversion. His bounded intervention removes a physical obstacle while somebody else's judgment determines the human outcome. Custom black sabers do not gain anti-magic or Darksaber status. |

Do not reveal these ten tracks as a flashback lecture. Each character first appears because a current action makes their prior choice matter. The initial footholds can exist in parallel without everybody meeting before R0.

## Native tracks and branch-placement decisions

Swain's Trifarix government is the selected governing context. The following are scoped staging proposals for this blueprint; they do not drag every published comic or cinematic onto the same date. Katarina does not require her post-comic blood-bond blade abilities. Riven's coerced return reaches Noxus on-page before any later arena status. Briar's escape from local confinement occurs on-page. Sion remains in imperial custody before a consequential deployment; he is not an obedient private guard.

| Native | Objective and specific action in this season | Cross-strand consequence and required reversal/payoff |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Swain | Determine whether the disrupted procurement route threatens imperial authority or exposes a useful rival. Requests limited information through an established observer, then offers conditional tolerance. | The Sins can accept one bounded transaction without accepting service. His information proves incomplete; he revises policy after concrete evidence rather than claiming every setback was planned. End result: formal limits on the particular private scheme, not total trust in the Sins. |
| LeBlanc | Preserve a private route to unusual subjects, research and leverage without exposing the whole network. Uses a plausible intermediary and a real offer. | She misreads one actor's willingness to be useful and loses a concrete local avenue. She can sacrifice that avenue without the text claiming all loss was secretly intended. Not identical to Swain or every aristocrat. |
| Darius | Keep public disorder from compromising military readiness and distinguish usable forces from uncontrollable liabilities. Orders a localized restriction after demonstrated disruption. | Restriction damages Dutch's supplies and the Sins' income. Evidence of diverted resources makes the private scheme a military problem too; he acts for Noxian interests, not sudden friendship. |
| Draven | Protect control of public acclaim and profitable spectacle. Challenges the Sins to prove a public claim on his terms and promotes Tai Lung. | The Sins' refusal and Tai Lung's later independent choice break the assumption that spectacle participants can be treated as inventory. His response changes reputation and access; he remains a capable dangerous figure, not comic humiliation. |
| Katarina | Trace a particular private operation that compromises her present duty to Noxus. Pursues a responsible actor, not the complete cast's secrets. | Her intervention collides with Matt's need for a living witness and Talon's distinct employer pressures. A limited agreement must define the person/object each is keeping, not generic alliance. |
| Talon | Preserve a Du Couteau interest and complete a specific recovery before public investigators reach it. He does not automatically obey Swain or Katarina. | His success in one recovery redirects Matt and changes the route the Sins attack. A later disagreement with Katarina determines whether the recovered resource can be used again. No omniscient tracking. |
| Riven | Retain agency and avoid having her skills repackaged as renewed consent. Arrives under coercive transfer; she is not already waiting as the gang's trainer. | The transfer intersects a Sins-selected strike and forces decisions about people traveling with the desired cargo. Her own refusal or intervention changes the escape; she does not owe permanent service for assistance. |
| Vladimir | Protect a profitable patronage/research investment while satisfying his own interests. Offers practical support that makes acceptance genuinely attractive. | A customer refuses an ownership claim, and evidence exposes a specific use of the works. He loses an actual local resource. No universal hemomancy of imported bodies is needed to establish threat. |
| Elise | Maintain access to people and elite rooms useful to her own predation and network. Moves a selected person through apparently desirable patronage. | That person's concrete absence is the human bridge between Jesse's local concern and Matt's case. Her influence is not automatically effective on a Sin; discovery changes her approach rather than revealing omnipotence. |
| Sion | An imperial deployment with intermittent remembered identity, not a conventional planner. The important authorial choice belongs to those releasing and directing him. | A disrupted route makes deployment dangerous to the very interests it was meant to protect. The decisive task becomes separating people and objectives, not every heavyweight lining up to punch him. Never require a new universal restraint or endless respawn. |
| Briar | Wants connection and control over her own choices. Her on-page escape creates immediate danger and an opportunity for somebody to offer ownership disguised as belonging. | She rejects a specific coercive offer and chooses a bounded action. Frank and the Sins must distinguish a dangerous person from the people exploiting her. Her agency supplies a route/decision unavailable through raw force. No compulsory membership or infantile portrayal. |
| Samira | Pursue demanding, enjoyable paid work while keeping her own terms. Takes a competing commission affecting the same property the Sins want. | Mutual interference proves each side's value and establishes a negotiated division for one job. Later she refuses a client's revised conditions, losing payment but changing an operation. Not designated gang instructor, subordinate or moral compass. |

## Twenty-four working episode cards

Each numbered entry is a concrete planning card. The bracketed act windows locate elapsed-time capacity; specific scene days can be assigned by the shared calendar. Sealed casualty decisions are deliberately absent.

### Act I — The trouble they choose [R0–10]

**1. First claim.** Deadpool's brief narration theft shows one embellished account and one stubborn visible consequence. Black Mimir reclaims ordinary narration. The Sins reach the capital district, recognize a limited Season 2 connection in a fighting company's public claim, and decide to take its stock/standing for their own reasons. They win usable goods and an immediate enemy. Draven hears of a challenge to the spectacle economy; an ordinary report, not supernatural omniscience, starts official attention.

**2. A place with a price.** The Sins turn some gain into temporary lodging and a practical base. Arthur discovers Dutch's actual camp through a verifiable familiar person, not coincidence at a universal reunion. Dutch offers a joint profitable action; Arthur wants contact but will not promise the Sins. In Zaun, Heisenberg evaluates a real Noxian inquiry through his Bilgewater foothold. Ryuk's substantial scene exposes the gap between Heisenberg's idea of loyalty and a business associate's actual choice; Ryuk gives no operational help.

**3. The public offer.** Draven offers a sanctioned contest to settle the reputation dispute on profitable terms. Kaze declines terms that would subordinate the gang and chooses a narrower, riskier public challenge; other Sins can refuse their part. Tai Lung insists on the value of his own earned ability. Samira's separate client loses access because of the disruption. The episode ends with tangible reward but a restricted route and named-in-scene institutional attention, not three unconnected introductions.

### Act II — Work on their own terms [R11–24]

**4. The first agreement.** The gang chooses its first paid job because it aligns with an existing grievance and gives it access to valuable equipment, not because an employer tells it why to visit Noxus. They negotiate scope and keep refusal rights. Samira is working for a conflicting claimant; a bounded contest becomes a division of objectives, not membership. Jesse earns his own local foothold and notices a specific worker harmed by products being sold under claims he recognizes.

**5. What travels home.** At R15, Matt negotiates the actual portal passage and reaches Zaun that day. The foreman has accepted payment to maintain a false voluntary-transfer account; a pending yard handover threatens to scatter the workers and remove him. Matt secures a living witness's account and a traceable commercial link; Jesse decides which evidence to entrust rather than simply delivering exposition. Talon's competing recovery denies them the easy route to the responsible party. Matt leaves for Zaun around R15 because the next necessary link is there. A Zaun scene shows Heisenberg trying to make a partner's dependence count as affection; Ryuk watches the failed substitution. No instant receipt of Matt's findings.

**6. Something worth keeping.** A brief Zaun consequence shows the foreman accepting the supplier's reward rather than merely hiding in fear. The Sins and selected members of Dutch's group complete a profitable action that supplies the camp and improves the Sins' base. It also damages the private works' supply schedule. Darius authorizes a local restriction in response to actual disorder; this raises everyone's cost of movement. Kaze recognizes that the apparent win strengthened a supplier's leverage over Dutch. Daniel detects pressure without pretending he knows the whole scheme. They keep the profit and inherit a harder choice.

### Act III — The price of access [R25–38]

**7. A promise of home.** A limited lead on travel knowledge brings Thor into the same property dispute. Mimir recognizes him in his own narration with brief personal shock and context; he does not tell the characters Thor's history through a nonexistent channel. Thor agrees only to a concrete service for access. The Sins' interference changes what he can obtain; he becomes an obstacle or negotiating party rather than an automatic ally. No functioning inter-universe portal is promised by the author.

**8. Useful information.** Heimdall appears because a negotiation depends on his assessment. Mimir recognizes him distinctly and reacts to the contradiction with remembered history, without knowing his exact extraction moment. Heimdall correctly perceives one participant's intention but overestimates what the participant actually knows. Swain's limited offer reaches the Sins through an established route; they accept or reject specific terms rather than swear loyalty. In Zaun, 47 refuses a changed contractual condition. A shown local Ryuk visit can expose Heisenberg's response; otherwise keep the choice with the two humans and let Ryuk's next owner-side scene contrast it.

**9. The third claimant.** A short case bridge commits Matt to intercepting the already-scheduled delivery before the yard handover; that action, not a voyage, occupies his Zaun block. Maul interferes with a works-bound consignment to obtain access and leverage. The Sins pursue their already-selected objective and find a rival operation, not a random crossover duel. Riddick controls a practical route or part of the disputed goods because of his own earlier action. A temporary three-way bargain succeeds only on its narrow terms. The surviving dispute exposes the works compound as the same place touching several supposedly unrelated opportunities.

### Act IV — People inside the bargain [R39–52]

**10. The altered load.** A planned Sins strike intersects Riven's coerced transfer. The gang wants a specific military/research capability tied to its grievance; Riven and other people are not the job's original bait. Frank refuses an indiscriminate solution, and the gang changes its approach by agreement. Riven's own resistance makes a difference. A short glimpse of Sion's imperial movement establishes a separate dangerous deployment, not another captive for the Sins to collect. Matt is already working the Zaun side; he has not acquired the later corroboration merely by arriving.

**11. The person behind the claim.** At R43–47 the delivery interception confronts the foreman with the consequences of the account he maintained; he chooses to contradict it. This supplies an independent human fact, not omniscient proof of every crime. Jesse follows the disappearance of the person previously introduced in his local work. Elise's patronage access supplies the route; Shang Tsung's claimed service supplies a misleading explanation. Jesse identifies a concrete discrepancy and acts on it without suddenly becoming an expert in sorcery. In Zaun, Matt tests the Noxian account against the living source and an actually intercepted delivery, after earlier work exposed the false voluntary-transfer story. Heisenberg's attempt to control how a confidant reads his conduct gives Ryuk an extended observer scene, not a notebook tutorial.

**12. Nobody's property.** The short Zaun bridge at R49–53 settles the threatened coworkers and earned-pay obligation, allowing the foreman to choose the R54 return. Briar's escape occurs during a disturbance at the local holding/research side of the scheme, with her restraint and choices determining the danger. The Sins have to decide what to do with a dangerous person during their own operation. A nearby attempt to order Starkiller as a hired instrument produces a separate violent refusal, opening a route while destroying an asset somebody needed. He is not Briar's owner or an instant hero. The works' private backers can no longer describe the disruption as a routine commercial dispute.

### Act V — Freedom with conditions [R53–66]

**13. Dutch's good deal.** Matt's R54 portal return immediately affects the main action: Arthur and camp members now have specific evidence with which to challenge the supplier's custody-coach demand. Dutch proposes a bargain that appears to solve the camp's material problem but lets a patron claim continuing service. The camp members have identifiable differing responses; Arthur cannot speak for all of them and refuses to make the Sins collateral. A practical operation makes the disagreement matter immediately. At the previously agreed R54 rendezvous, Matt and the consenting source use Kaze's valid portal back. Their return supplies evidence and a person with choices, not an automatic resolution of the Noxian case.

**14. A quieter kind of refusal.** Matt and Jesse prevent a further transfer, intersecting Magneto's local concern without making him their automatic protector. Magneto intervenes over one concrete property/person demand after initially staying apart. The Sins exploit the opening for their own objective; he rejects being credited as their member or the leader of an all-Elsewhered movement. Vladimir's attractive patronage offer becomes visibly conditional. In Zaun, 47 protects one contracted interest while leaving a different problem outside his terms; Heisenberg must absorb the limit. Ryuk observes the tension between paid effectiveness and the devotion Heisenberg wants.

**15. The public cost.** Matt/Jesse's already-active corroboration helps cost Shang Tsung a client resource; the case is useful before card 19. Draven and the private house attempt to restore confidence with a demonstration. Tai Lung refuses a last-minute change that treats his accomplishment as someone else's property; Samira separately rejects altered client terms. The Sins turn the disrupted event into a gain, but their chosen violence makes ordinary access and camp supply harder. Jesse's earlier discrepancy now costs Shang Tsung a concrete client resource. Darius's response is a targeted operational restriction, not collective punishment presented as a neutral moral good.

### Act VI — Who pays when they refuse? [R67–80]

**16. The works fight back.** The counterattack responds in part to those prior losses, and Matt protects the specific witness caught in it. The gang defends something it chose to keep; Billy have active roles suited to their real abilities. Frank leads the agreed portion only. Chris must preserve maintenance capacity while the others deny access to the intruders. The attack costs the house a capability and costs the Sins comfort or freedom of action; neither loss is secretly meaningless.

**17. The offer behind the offer.** Shang's planted account attacks Matt and Jesse's actual gains; they must expose the new lie without overstating what their own evidence proves. LeBlanc's intermediary proposes a genuine short-term advantage in return for an unacceptable future claim. Maul tries to turn the same pressure into dependence on him. Kaze can recognize the hustle and still want part of the benefit. Daniel identifies manipulation but cannot make the material dilemma vanish. In Zaun, a reduced or rerouted foreign order changes Heisenberg's plan; Singed bargains from his own usefulness. Ryuk observes that scientific respect does not equal friendship or obedience.

**18. What each one wants.** The primary action is the Sins recovering their diverted carriage while Thor seizes the promised travel material for himself after learning it offers no verified way home. He wants his family and realm, not membership in a liberation cause. The one substantial secondary follows Heimdall pursuing the information holder more harshly: his demand is access to Odin, and he is willing to impose hierarchy rather than reject it. A short consequence carries forward Starkiller's separate paid coercive task and later dispute over its terms; do not stage a third full refusal sequence here. These actions disrupt different portions of the private operators' plan and do not form a heavyweight coalition. Matt is back in Noxus, protecting the source while Jesse establishes the matching local account.

### Act VII — The cost comes due [R81–98]

**19. What the case changes.** At R82, a new recovery attempt responds to the case's earlier successes; Matt acts on corroboration obtained during his earlier Zaun block and the source who has been in Noxus since the R54 return. Talon's new recovery attempt and Katarina's intervention—not a belated arrival—force the present collision. Jesse determines what happens with the Noxian half. Katarina's pursuit of an actor and Talon's recovery objective collide with the need to preserve that person; a precise negotiated boundary or deliberate refusal changes the next action. The evidence now makes one private operation politically expensive to protect. It is not a paperwork raid or an automatic courtroom victory.

**20. A weapon on the wrong road.** Earlier interference disrupts a real imperial deployment route involving Sion. Darius's responsibility, the private house's attempt to exploit the movement, and the Sins' objective no longer align. The episode is about separating people, cargo and the dangerous force from one another across established geography, not an all-cast boss fight. Riven makes a consequential decision about how her ability is used. In Zaun, the notebook strand shows limits Heisenberg cannot simply erase. An owner-side Ryuk scene can contrast Caitlyn's decision to keep the book unused with that demand for control; it does not give her secret knowledge of his latest plans. Exact targets/results remain undisclosed.

**21. The chosen job.** The gang chooses a final paid operation whose objective also settles its own local grievance: break the private works' coercive capacity and recover the assets/people it has personally committed to, not overthrow the empire. They dictate scope, define who can refuse, and do not pledge themselves to Swain or LeBlanc. Samira's participation, Riddick's route and Deadpool's specific knowledge are separately negotiated or independently motivated. Dutch cannot promise his entire camp's participation; the members shown act individually.

### Act VIII — What they will not sell [R99–126]

**22. The split objective.** The final operation begins across previously established local spaces. One Sins group contests the active capability; another protects its own people and the independent participant whose choice determines whether the coercion can continue. Chris/Billy state is scheduled explicitly so both are not simultaneously available as separate bodies. Separate native/crossover actions open or close particular routes, rather than appearing by roll-call. The operation breaks one material claim and leaves another unresolved for the next card.

**23. Keep the win.** The counterstroke aims to turn the Sins' success into somebody else's ownership or political credit. Kaze and the gang choose a cost they will accept to reject that conversion. Matt and Jesse preserve the human consequence of the case; the native authorities decide what they will tolerate for their own reasons. In Zaun, the last substantial Ryuk scene examines Heisenberg's next response to a foreign loss and a relationship he cannot compel. The empire adjusts and survives as a continuing thread; the loss is not erased by a secret master plan.

**24. Their own terms.** Pay the material and emotional bills: who controls the works' former capacity, which routes reopened or remain lost, what the gang repaired or abandoned, and what its reputation now buys. The Sins have become a self-directed mercenary gang through choices already seen, retaining their independent Zaun stake without receiving Noxian citizenship as a reward for goodness. Ordinary life and contact with Dutch's people show the cost of chosen belonging. Heisenberg remains a regional criminal power with a contested foreign reach. The season closes its specific ownership struggle and leaves genuinely changed relationships; no compulsory all-cast departure or next-season roster is invented.

## Concrete objects, jobs and antagonistic decisions

This register supplies the selected physical referents and terms for the cards above. These are committed prospective authoring decisions, not new powers or published Noxian facts.

- **The recurring works:** an outer-city armor-and-carriage works with three separately controllable functions: a warehouse of imported performance products; a forge/repair floor producing conventional arms and reinforced transport frames; and a guarded yard where contracted fighters and coerced laborers are housed. The frames are useful against ordinary bodies, not universal restraints for the imported cast. Its owners promise timely private force and equipment to competing buyers. Vladimir supplies a particular patronage investment; the Black Rose seeks access to research and people; neither automatically owns every buyer or the imperial army.
- **Opening gain:** the Sins seize a reserve of weapons and equipment from expedition-linked profiteers after deciding the group is worth confronting. They sell part and keep part. They do not receive an instant unchallengeable deed to an entire district. The observed connection to Ambessa's expedition is planted in Season 2 and recognized again, not learned through a new narrator power.
- **First paid client/objective:** an independent local weaponsmith offers payment to recover equipment the same private house has withheld. The gang likes the existing enemy and the recoverable value. Samira's separate client wants a particular consignment transferred instead; the agreed division gives her that consignment only after the smith's identified property is separated. Nobody receives blanket permission to seize civilian possessions.
- **The human case:** Jesse befriends a previously introduced worker through his own paid local work. The worker accepts a better-paying opportunity through elite patronage and then becomes inaccessible. Jesse discovers that the stated destination and actual work site differ by following a physical delivery/contact, not by magically diagnosing sorcery. Matt needs both the worker's account and the linked Zaun supplier's corroboration; neither document alone proves the entire scheme.
- **First material reversal:** the successful joint action in card 6 removes the works' reserve shipment, but the resulting local restriction leaves Dutch's camp dependent on a supplier tied to the same patronage circle. Kaze spots the change because prices and available routes change in front of him. Dutch accepts credit before the full long-term service demand is presented.
- **Riven's transfer:** the gang attacks the transport-frame/equipment consignment it has already traced. Her coerced transfer is using that actual escorted movement. She refuses a proposed violent shortcut because it endangers another person being moved and changes the escape herself. Frank's objection is independently grounded in the same immediate risk, not instant knowledge of her biography.
- **Briar's transfer and escape:** the private scheme tries to divert a limited imperial custody movement into its own holding yard under a disputed work/research instruction. The Sins' existing raid disrupts the movement; Briar chooses and acts to escape. Swain is not assumed to know or approve the diversion. This creates an actual government-versus-private-interest dispute rather than claiming she was always a privately owned laboratory fixture.
- **Thor's one service:** clear a blocked approach so the claimed travel archive can be reached. The source may contain genuine local planar history without containing a route to his home. He performs an agreed physical task and then discovers the patron has sold certainty it does not possess. No new portal is created by the outline.
- **Heimdall's consequential error:** he establishes that an intermediary sincerely intends to provide the promised material; he then treats that sincerity as evidence of the material's reliability. The intermediary's belief is wrong. This preserves the ability's value while making his judgment, contempt and dependence on Odin matter. His next choice is harsher pursuit of the actual information holder, not humble conversion into a team player.
- **Tai Lung's darker choice:** before the later refusal, he accepts a higher-profile performance opportunity that exploits the disruption and eclipses another competitor. This harms somebody else's livelihood and feeds his grievance-driven appetite for recognition. His later attack on the patron's claim is possessive and prideful, not a sudden altruistic awakening.
- **Starkiller's earlier complicity:** the first refusal does not mean every subsequent choice is liberatory. Between cards 12 and 18, he accepts a bounded coercive security task because service remains familiar and materially useful. The consequences make his later refusal costly and personal. Do not replace him with heroic Galen wearing different armor.
- **Magneto's bounded commitment:** defend the residence and immediate dependents whose shelter was made conditional on service, not establish an instant nation. The Sins benefit from the resulting opening but cannot advertise his support. His disappointment in grand causes does not prohibit one concrete commitment.
- **Final client and payment:** the independent workshops/carriers damaged by the private house pool a practical fee and a share of recoverable equipment. The Sins choose the job because it also settles their own grievance and protects their own base. The client does not direct their personal allegiance. Ordinary people pooling money are an accepted client class, not a new named crossover faction.
- **Final split objectives:** stop the yard's current coercive transfer; deny the warehouse/transport combination the ability to continue that transfer; preserve the independent person's opportunity to refuse; recover the specific equipment the gang agreed to recover. A fight won at the gate does not complete all four. Sion's earlier deployment crisis is not the same operation as this final raid.
- **The explicit cost of keeping the win:** the gang destroys a valuable reserve of seized arms/transport stock rather than let the patron use ownership of that stock to bind the people and the gang to continuing service. They still earn their agreed fee and retain a defensible local base, but lose the much larger future profit they could have taken as that patron's enforcers. Their decision comes from independence and attachment, not a blanket turn to civic virtue.
- **Political resolution:** Swain/Darius curtail the specific private diversion because it competes with imperial control. The Sins obtain a bounded right to operate and keep their demonstrated claim; no eternal immunity or universal state endorsement follows. LeBlanc and Vladimir lose a real local avenue/investment without the narrator calling every setback their plan.
- **Zaun resolution:** Heisenberg loses one Noxian channel and preserves his Zaun base and Bilgewater foothold. He switches to a narrower foreign approach using existing contacts, not a reset that instantly restores the lost channel. 47 gets paid under the agreed scope and keeps independent maintenance. The notebook cannot purchase trust, supply a foreign market's missing practical capacity, or make Ryuk a loyal adviser.

### Exact interfaces and active villain counter-moves

The following specifics supersede abbreviated alternatives in the earlier episode cards and tables.

- **Opening stolen stock:** twelve crates of conventional swords, spearheads and armor fittings, two repair wagons, and the expedition-linked company's cash reserve. The gang attacks an armed profiteering operation it has identified, not an innocent random household. Keeping and selling useful stolen property makes them dangerous outlaws; witnesses and owners can reasonably oppose them. Frank refuses a needless punishment of a noncombatant and his refusal is respected.
- **First job (card 4):** the local carriage smith wants two wagon-mounted ballistae and four replacement winch assemblies returned from the works' loading court. His mark and an established withheld-delivery dispute supply the limited basis for the claim. He offers a quarter of the recovered equipment's agreed resale value in cash, with half the fee in advance. The Sins accept only this recovery and retain a refusal right if the client adds a person as cargo. Samira's client wants the neighboring six-crate weapon consignment; she is not trying to steal the smith's property. They negotiate separate loading access under fire, producing competition and cooperation within the same immediate action.
- **Dutch's bargain (cards 6/13):** an existing works supplier advances four weeks of camp food, bedding and repair materials against three guarded freight movements and standby defense of its yard. The advertised cargo is armor and machinery; one later movement includes custody coaches. Dutch argues the altered job is the price of getting the camp secure. Hosea contests the terms; Charles and John refuse to treat people as freight; Micah favors the profitable hard line; other participants make choices consistent with their own immediate stakes, not an imported late-game betrayal. Arthur refuses to make either his new family or the whole camp answer for Dutch's signature. Failure costs supplies and forfeits pledged equipment, not a magical ownership right over every camp member.
- **Jesse's local work and the missing person (cards 4/11):** he takes paid maintenance/hauling work at the gang's lodging yard without becoming its subordinate. The recurring worker is its adult shift cook, who accepts a better-paid kitchen position in a patron's residence. The cook instead appears on a guarded food delivery to the works, exhausted and unable to leave freely. Jesse recognizes the person and the imported product's sales claims; he does not need to identify a magical mechanism to challenge the stated story. The cook's later account and the supplying foreman's account in Zaun establish the concrete human/commercial chain Matt follows.
- **Talon's recovery (cards 5/19):** he retrieves the supplier's courier and the courier's stamped brass dispatch seal for a Du Couteau interest. The seal authenticates particular loading instructions; it is neither a magic key nor proof of every crime. Matt loses the easiest witness in card 5 and must travel for independent corroboration. In card 19, Talon wants the seal back while Katarina pursues the party using it to compromise her duty. Nobody needs an omniscient ledger raid, and the person can contradict the document.
- **Maul's concrete attempted win (cards 9/17):** he takes the works' dispatcher alive long enough to secure three contact introductions and an agreed freight diversion, then withholds the dispatcher's safe departure to demand continuing access. He turns the Sins' pressure into an attempt to build his own dependent network. His counterstrike diverts a reserve carriage to the works instead of the gang's chosen buyer, depriving the Sins of profit and increasing his negotiating position. His proposal of freedom therefore actively creates dependence; he is not another benevolent refusal arc. Any coercive Force use requires only already evidenced ordinary capability and cannot supply unknown memories of Runeterra.
- **Shang Tsung's concrete attempted win (cards 11/15/17):** he sells the patron a limited, demonstrated service while falsely marketing that success as proof he can solve the works' broader personnel problem. When Jesse exposes the discrepancy, Shang redirects blame to a rival local practitioner and offers the patron Jesse's public discrediting as the next service. The author does not grant him control of Jesse's mind or a copied private biography. His attack uses staged appearances, social access and a deliberately planted false account; a genuinely observed fact ultimately undermines it. His ambition worsens the risk to the worker and the investigation instead of merely withdrawing from ownership.
- **Sion's deployment (cards 10/20):** Darius's forces move him in an existing imperial war wagon toward a muster outside the capital as a planned military resource. Earlier district restrictions close the ordinary heavy-carriage approach. A private dispatcher then routes the wagon through the works' service road so the house can pressure officials over access and recover a detained shipment. That selfish rerouting exposes inhabited streets and interferes with the Sins' planned route. No new remote control, rune command or obedient Sion is assumed. The episode's victory conditions are clear civilians from the known approach, remove the commercial blockage, and prevent the private operators exploiting the confusion; the exact violent outcome remains spoiler-protected.
- **Final client/job (card 21):** the already introduced carriage smith and two affected carriers pool a fee equal to the first job's fee plus a one-third share of recoverable conventional stock. No new named faction or champion hires the gang. Target: the works' loading yard, transport frames and warehouse dispatch capacity that support coercive transfers. Recover the smith's remaining marked equipment and the carriers' wagons; protect the particular cook and other people the gang explicitly chooses to include. Refusal: no open-ended service, no indiscriminate destruction of occupied housing, no surrendering Briar/Riven or another independent person as substitute payment. The Sins choose this job for profit, revenge and their own claim; they are not bound to liberate Noxus.
- **Finite physical ending:** the gang burns or wrecks the captured arms reserve and empty transport frames after removing the agreed property and people, sacrificing the much larger resale prize to prevent the patron reclaiming the working coercive system. The forge building remains; ordinary smiths and carriers can resume work under a contested new arrangement. This denies a capability without magical industrial reset or gratuitous destruction of an entire civilian district. Personal casualty assignments remain undisclosed.
- **Foreign-order consequence and delay:** the first restriction around R20 delays a scheduled shipment, and a works-supplier notice travels capital to Bilgewater in the selected default 33 days, then Bilgewater to Zaun in 22 days. Its R75 arrival supplies the specific news in card 17, staged at approximately R76. The failed public demonstration around R60 separately stops the works' planned renewal of its imported-performance-product order. That notice reaches the Bilgewater agent around R93 and Heisenberg around R115, supplying card 23. Earlier Zaun scenes concern an initial Noxian inquiry, committed first-order terms and different known local matters—not premature knowledge of either later event. These are explicitly fictional production travel defaults, not official timetables. This delay creates dramatic irony rather than instant synchronized administration.
- **Heisenberg's concrete countermove:** instead of restoring the broken works channel by authorial fiat, he refuses its unsecured replacement order and instructs his Bilgewater contact to sell the retained stock to existing non-Noxian buyers while seeking one smaller Noxian customer. That preserves income but loses the large channel and its prestige. 47 guards the contracted local stock/associate in Zaun and declines a retroactive obligation to solve the foreign political failure. Singed insists his useful research does not guarantee customers or obedience. If this is one of Ryuk's shown local visits, he observes Heisenberg describing independent choices as betrayal. No remote observation or permanent borrower attachment is implied.

For clinical fictional-product effects, altered body compatibility and supernatural confrontations, the existing power owner still supplies the specific established rule. These concrete staging choices do not authorize recipes, new powers, universal restraints or invisible communication channels.

## Core gang and carryover guarantees

- **Kaze:** drives self-chosen confrontations and bargains, reads social incentives, and can knowingly choose danger; no new BLACKLIGHT weapons or elasticity. His street intelligence and Daniel's insight must affect the selected route rather than merely appear in narration.
- **Chris/Billy:** one continuity requires explicit transformation scheduling. Chris's competence and chosen care underpin Frank's maintenance; Billy's horror, predatory autonomy and accepted immortality remain. The industrial conflicts cannot assume Billy loses consciousness or that a generic cage contains him.
Base defense, route control and protecting their own give them repeated initiative; their prior experience changes what terms the gang will accept. No second kidnap imposed to manufacture stakes.
- **Daniel:** reads manipulation, proposes a materially different option, and can still choose an ugly compromise. Not a universal truth engine.
- **Arthur:** has both new-family membership and a real, unresolved old-family bond. His upgraded body and branch knowledge are retained; Dutch's approval is not the source of his agency.
- **Frank:** an equal member, voluntary upgrades by Chris alone, leads only by agreement, retains independent limits. Kaze does not attack him to resolve disagreement. Frank refusing one action need not mean expulsion or betrayal.
- **Jesse:** independent local work, recognition of a concrete harm, a risky choice about evidence, and a final human consequence. His survival is protected; he is not an expendable witness or nothing but a guilt reaction to Walt.
- **Daredevil:** approved Cox presentation and ordinary unpossessed Waid-era sensory/acrobatic emphasis; three physical-location blocks, no heavyweight body copied from Frank, no objective lie detector. His case changes what protagonists can do.
- **Heisenberg/47/Singed/Ryuk:** all four retain distinct agency. The cancer cure remains genuine and already achieved by the end of Season 2. 47's maintenance is independent. Singed has his own terms. Ryuk has eight relationship-centered opportunities (2, 5, 8, 11, 14, 17, 20, 23), subject to the owner/contact/local-visit ledger above, not a compulsory appearance schedule or permanent attachment to Heisenberg. Each Zaun cut changes a foreign decision or the reader's understanding of a relationship that will change it.
- **Native Season 2 carryovers:** Vi, Jinx, Caitlyn, Ekko, Heimerdinger, Jayce, Mel, Sevika, Firelights and Jinxers do not all relocate by default. Their known Season 2 consequences remain in the Zaun state ledger. A return visit or message is used only if its sender's current knowledge and actual task warrant it. No unselected death/survival is asserted to fill a gap.

## Knowledge, body and narration checks before episode execution

1. Mimir's recognition of Thor/Heimdall comes from his accepted history and stays in narration. His different emotional reactions orient a new listener; he cannot know the selected extraction instant or unseen local activity on sight. Deadpool's hijack remains brief and is not a universal communications power.
2. Material advantages may connect plots; nonexistent shared power equivalence may not. No universal rune suppression, mind-reading omniscience, metal-control omnipotence, soul compatibility or lightsaber anti-magic is required by this blueprint.
3. Place Riven, Briar, Katarina, Talon and Sion at the scoped branch stages above in the common calendar before writing their first scene. These are committed prospective authoring placements, not claims that publication order is chronology.
4. The notebook's selected audience-ahead viewpoint remains. Exact possession, eligible identity, face, knowledge, and result must be entered by the separate custody/Death Note owner. No later cameo silently sees Ryuk without the required contact, and no state-wide compliance follows from one notebook entry.
5. Every paid job has an objective, client interest, agreed scope, refusal point, payment and actual downside. A military/private/civilian client category is not prior consent to any atrocity. The gang's dangerous character does not erase individual autonomy.
6. Ordinary counterplay comes before exceptional powers. If a decisive beat requires an unsettled power interaction, stage a physical/material alternative rather than silently invent a neutralizer.
7. The new viewer learns place, current objective, obstacle and changed consequence before a cut. A returning face gets the relevant relationship, not its full franchise biography. A set-piece may be brutal without becoming spatially confusing.


## Scale, motive and Deadpool corrections applied to this outline

### A powerful cast does not spend a season struggling with a gate

The works is a recurring economic and social stake, **not a 24-episode invulnerable fortress**. Early raids succeed at their actual limited objectives. Buildings and ordinary guards provide only the resistance their real capabilities allow. the story follows the resulting loss, displacement, retaliation, rival acquisition and relationship changes.

The continuing questions are who they choose to protect or exploit, which property they want intact, what they actually know, who can refuse, and what their victory permits someone else to do. Do not hide a known target or invent a magic shield to consume episode slots. The works loses stock in cards 1/4/6, custody/control in 10/12, clients in 15, an offensive capacity in 16 and political cover in 19 before the final disputed transfer. Each is a lasting change. The ending resolves the remaining operation, not the first breach of the same gate.

No scene requires all imported characters to be equally strong. Some confrontations end quickly. Tactical competence, social power, supernatural power and willingness to devastate a place are different things.

### Deadpool's single causal through-line

During the offscreen interval, Wade accepts paid escort work for what is represented as recovered equipment. At the works' loading court he discovers people are being moved under the same claim. He breaks the agreement, gets some of them out and keeps money the employer says he owes. He does not destroy the whole business or know every captive route.

- **Card 1:** the aggrieved local is the existing works loading supervisor from that job, not a new unrelated fan. Wade's boast that he settled everything is contradicted by that person's continuing authority.
- **Cards 3/6:** Wade tries to hide his unfinished obligation behind the Sins' public disruption. His misleading directions cost them access to the easy loading approach; they demand the truth.
- **Card 11/12:** the familiar court helps connect the present disappearance to his own prior job. He admits exactly what he saw and what he did not investigate; this gives Jesse a usable lead, not a full map of Noxus.
- **Card 16:** he stays for a dangerous portion of the base defense instead of using the uproar to leave his debt behind. His regenerative capability works without repairing trust by itself.
- **Card 21/22:** his contribution is the ordinary service entrance and current recognition risk he actually knows. The loading supervisor recognizes him, so his old experience creates both access and a complication. He has to follow through with the agreed people/task rather than narrate himself a victory.

The brief opening narration theft is comic presentation, not his entire subplot. He wants connection and recognition, uses jokes to evade discomfort, can make a selfish mistake, and later acts when the joke will no longer do the work. He does not become another permanent Sin or the omniscient author.

### Different answers to Noxus, not ten identical emancipation arcs

- **Thor** wants a route to his family and realm; frustration, destructive capacity and learned obedience complicate that desire. He is not automatically a recruit or a benevolent champion.
- **Heimdall** wants access to Odin and confirmation of the hierarchy he believes in. He can correctly read intention and still draw a contemptuous, mistaken inference about knowledge.
- **Tai Lung** wants recognition and supremacy. His refusal of a patron can hurt rivals and feed his entitlement rather than redeem him.
- **Maul** uses apparent shared liberation to construct dependence. His hostage/contact maneuver and diverted carriage actively cost the Sins something.
- **Shang Tsung** seeks status through useful service, exaggeration and fraud. Exposure produces a countermove against Jesse's credibility, not passive withdrawal.
- **Lord Starkiller** can reject one controller while accepting coercive work for another. One act of refusal does not turn the actual Sith Stalker into heroic Galen.
- **Magneto** makes a bounded commitment to a place and actual people while resisting an unwanted grand cause. Local mages are not automatically his world's mutants.
- **Dutch** sells restriction as the route to future freedom; the camp's disagreement becomes material rather than a copied late-game betrayal.
- **Riddick** keeps independent movement and survival central. A shared exit is a bargain, not adoption into the ensemble's moral project.
- **Deadpool** has to distinguish being applauded from actually staying for someone.

## Native operational consequences that survive the finale

Swain gets narrower private military competition, not obedient Sins. Darius restores a usable military approach but has to account for the diversion that endangered it. Draven loses exclusive control of this particular spectacle's meaning, not all credibility. LeBlanc loses a local access channel; Vladimir loses an investment; neither secretly wanted every loss. Katarina's objective and Talon's Du Couteau obligation remain distinct. Riven and Briar retain meaningful refusal, not debts of lifelong service. Samira's independent terms and lost payment remain real. Elise's exposed human route becomes less usable, not a universal end to predation. Sion's actual deployment consequences are accounted for without an invented remote command or guaranteed revival.

# Release standard and checkpoint

## Narration for a new viewer

Black Mimir remains the first-person ordinary narrator. Give the audience the immediate place, relevant relationship, present desire and obstacle before a cut. Explain a consequential ability or limitation when it changes a choice, not through a biography before action.

His storytelling uses a concrete hook, a change in what the listener assumes, and a consequence worth remembering. Humor can precede trouble; grief or cruelty need not receive a punchline. Use readable Scottish-flavored vocabulary and conversational rhythm sparingly, not phonetic caricature, constant catchphrases or invented audio measurements.

He recognizes Thor and Heimdall from personal history and reacts differently to each. Their presence can genuinely interrupt his account. He does not know their extraction instants or unseen Noxian careers merely by seeing them. Narration creates neither a traveling Mimir body nor a channel into the actors' minds. Deadpool's brief accepted hijack ends; Mimir resumes.

Quiet scenes stay brief but retain the moment a decision changes. Shortening explanation is not permission to remove motive, human consequence or spatial clarity.

No previously inaccessible audio or comic interior is declared newly verified.
