# KAZE VS. MULTIVERSE — Acts VII–VIII repair

Repair authorized 2026-10-05, Pacific/Honolulu. Latest correction: **keep the early serum**.

This is a replacement delivery edition, not story acceptance or a new source release. The accepted reboot baseline remains PRE-PLAY with zero accepted reboot episodes. The selected repaired continuation ends after VIII.3 below; next unwritten is IX.1. No IX prose is included.

## Scope and edition selection

- Preserve the early serum sourcing, successful administration and Chris's gains in VII.2, followed by the evolved Billy/depot reveal in VII.3. The initial proposal to move these to VIII.2–3 is withdrawn. Do not administer the serum or reveal Billy's growth a second time.
- Preserve VII's Warwick/Vander activation and custody, Silco's death, the on-page Smeech contract and assassination, and the depot outcomes. Preserve VIII.1's Frank encounter, barge destruction, machinery recovery, and endpoint with Kaze and Vi departing for the Last Drop.
- Apply the narrow VII.1–VII.2 Viktor/Sky relocation below. It changes when the fatal experiment is presented; it does not cure Viktor, restore Sky after her death, change the serum's sources, or add an extra experiment.
- Replace VIII.2 and VIII.3 with the complete texts below. The original VIII.2 rocket construction and roof approach, and the entire original VIII.3 rocket interception/arrest confrontation, are superseded.
- Superseded consequences supply no current knowledge or state: no rocket fired, no interception, no destroyed launcher, no Jinx attack admission, no attack-caused warrant or workshop standoff, no confiscated Zapper, no fresh Vi split lip from that fight, no Jinx asylum compact arising from it, and no attack-caused suspension of a Council hearing.
- Cassandra's survival at this endpoint is the ordinary pre-attack condition. Her planned IX.2 death and Mel's instinctive protection remain future outcomes requiring the actual attack and physical circumstances.

## VII.1–VII.2 local continuity patch

The full literal VII.1 and VII.2 transcripts were not recoverable as local files. Retrieved conversation evidence identifies the fatal experiment as early VII material and the serum administration in VII.2. This section is an explicit scene-level replacement, not a claim that missing prose was recovered verbatim or that the historical chat messages were edited.

**VII.1:** retain the delivered Warwick/Singed/Vi/Jinx material, the bounded research result, Viktor's health crisis and his acquisition of Shimmer. Replace the prematurely completed Hexcore self-experiment, Sky's death and all ash/altered-body aftermath with this ending:

---

Viktor returned to the laboratory with the vial hidden inside his coat.

Sky looked up from the table. Her notes lay beneath one hand; the other held the cloth she had been using to clean an instrument.

“You should be resting.”

“I tried.”

He set his cane against the bench. His fingers remained around it for a moment after its tip found the floor.

Sky saw that. She pulled a chair closer without remarking on it.

Viktor sat.

Across the room, the Hexcore waited inside its apparatus. The shapes upon it shifted slowly, each alteration leaving him less certain that the last one had been an accident.

Sky followed his gaze.

“We still don't know what it does to a living system.”

“We know what doing nothing will do to mine.”

She put down the cloth.

“Those aren't the only two choices.”

Viktor looked at her then. He seemed about to answer, but a cough caught him first. He turned aside, pressed his handkerchief to his mouth and waited for it to pass.

Sky stayed until his breathing settled.

“I'll leave the measurements here,” she said. “Read them before you touch anything.”

He nodded.

When she had gone, Viktor unfolded the handkerchief.

The blood had reached a clean corner.

He sat for a long while with the little vial in his hand. Then he pulled Sky's notes toward him and began to read.

---

**VII.2:** replace any opening that already treats Sky as dead with the following single fatal-experiment scene. It precedes the retained serum-sourcing/action sequence. Preserve the independently earned material acquisition, Chris's administration, Walter/Jesse control conflict and 47 contract setup afterward. Remove duplicate Sky-death scenes and redundant retrospective death summaries elsewhere in VII.2.

---

The laboratory had become quiet enough for Viktor to hear the clock between his breaths.

Sky's notes were open beside him.

He had read them. That mattered, though it did not make what he chose any less his choice.

He used the Shimmer Singed had given him, waited through its violent passage beneath his skin, and brought himself into contact with the living experiment.

The Hexcore answered.

Light followed the veins of his hand. It climbed where ordinary light should have stopped, beneath the skin and around the bones. Viktor gripped the bench with his other hand. His injured leg drew taut.

For one astonished breath, the pain changed.

Then the apparatus shook.

Viktor tried to withdraw. His arm did not follow the rest of him. The connection held, drawing him forward as the shape inside the chamber folded through another impossible arrangement.

Sky heard the impact from the passage.

She came through the door and saw him bent against the table, his hand caught inside the light.

“Viktor!”

She reached the supply control and pulled it down.

The room's ordinary lamps dimmed.

The light around his arm did not.

Sky looked from the control to Viktor. He was trying to speak, but his jaw had locked. She saw his knees begin to give.

She went to him.

“I'm here.”

Her hand closed over his shoulder. She braced herself against the bench and tried to pull him free.

The light crossed into her.

Viktor saw her face change before he understood why. He tried to turn toward her. His trapped hand flexed once inside the connection, and the chamber answered with a sharp, rising sound.

Sky's grip loosened.

Her fingers broke apart against his coat.

He caught at her with his free hand.

There was nothing solid left to catch.

Ash moved through the light where she had stood. Her clothes fell against the table and slid to the floor.

The connection released him.

Viktor struck the ground on his side. He dragged himself toward the clothes, leaving the cane where it had fallen.

“Sky.”

He lifted a sleeve. Dust fell from its fold.

“Sky.”

His changed hand looked wrong against the fabric. He turned it over as if the answer might be there, then pressed it flat against the floor to stop its trembling.

The Hexcore continued to move behind him.

Viktor did not look at it.

He gathered what he could with the edge of the cloth. His first attempt spilled through his fingers, and he made himself slow down.

No one came through the door.

He had wanted enough time to live.

Now he sat on the floor with hers upon his hands.

---

No restricted donor or source route is newly supplied by this patch. The retrieved early-serum history remains the source for the Extremis programme records and sealed reference material, Earth-1610 S.H.I.E.L.D. ocular archive, preserved Teemo material, compatibility work and actual administration. Exact unavailable original sentences remain unavailable, not reconstructed evidence.

## Act VIII — Episode 2/3 — replacement edition

The Last Drop had extinguished its sign.

Without the colour spilling across the street, the place looked narrower. Wet brick, iron shutters, a door with fresh scratches around the lock. Somebody had swept the broken glass into the gutter and left the blood on the step.

Vi reached for the handle.

A bolt scraped behind it.

“Closed.”

“Sevika.”

A pause. Then the inspection hatch opened.

The woman on the other side looked at Vi, looked past her at Kaze, and shut the hatch again. I heard someone object before the bolt came back.

Sevika opened the door just wide enough.

“If you're here to start something—”

“I came for my sister.”

“That makes two things I don't need tonight.”

But she let them in.

The bar smelled of stale beer and the sharp cleaning spirits somebody had used upstairs. Chairs stood on tables. Four men occupied a booth beneath the balcony, their coats still on, weapons within reach. A fifth stood behind the bar with both hands planted on a ledger.

Kaze noticed the weapons. The men noticed him noticing.

He smiled a little.

Vi kept walking.

“Where is she?”

“Office.”

Sevika caught her arm before she reached the stairs.

“Don't promise her something you can't give.”

Vi looked down at the hand.

Then back up.

Sevika released her.

Kaze followed Vi halfway across the floor before the man behind the bar spoke.

“You.”

Kaze stopped.

The man had a swollen cheek and a strip of cloth tied around his left hand.

“You were at the freight yard.”

Kaze glanced at the bandage.

“You should've stayed there.”

“That stock belonged to us.”

“Did it?”

“We worked for him.”

The man pointed toward the ceiling.

Above us, a floorboard creaked.

Kaze's expression lost its amusement.

“Lower your fucking voice.”

The man looked at Sevika, seeking something—permission, perhaps, or reassurance that the world still had rules he understood.

She gave him neither.

“Sit down,” she said.

He didn't.

Vi had already reached the office door.

* * *

Jinx sat on the floor beside Silco's desk.

His chair was empty.

Someone had moved his body out of the room, but his coat remained folded over its back, one sleeve hanging lower than the other. Jinx held the little apparatus he had used for his eye. She turned it between her fingers without looking at it.

Vi stopped inside the doorway.

“Hey.”

Jinx pressed the apparatus shut.

Click.

Opened it.

Click.

“Busy.”

“I saw Vander.”

The clicking stopped.

Vi came farther in, leaving the door open behind her.

“He remembered me.”

Jinx looked up.

There was anger in that look before there was hope. Hope had become a thing she wanted to inspect for concealed weapons.

“What did he say?”

“My name.”

“Convenient.”

“He asked where you were.”

Jinx's thumb slipped. The apparatus snapped against her finger.

She stared at the tiny red mark.

Vi crouched.

“He's still in there.”

“Which part?”

“Powder—”

“No.” Jinx's eyes lifted sharply. “Which part, Vi? The part that knows us? The part with the teeth? The part that's going to remember everything and ask what happened?”

Vi's mouth tightened.

“I don't know.”

That answer held Jinx's attention.

A worse answer might have sounded kinder.

Vi looked at the coat.

“I'm not asking you to stop missing him.”

Jinx followed her gaze.

For a moment, neither sister moved.

Then Jinx said, very quietly, “He was trying to kill you.”

“I know.”

“I didn't mean—”

Her mouth closed.

Vi reached out, stopped short of touching her, and left her hand resting on her own knee.

Downstairs, a chair scraped.

The noise pulled Jinx's eyes toward the door.

“What's he doing here?”

“Kaze?”

“No, the fucking furniture.”

“He brought me.”

“Of course he did.”

“He said he'd help if Vander lost control.”

Jinx studied her sister's face.

“And you believe him?”

“About that.”

Aye. There was a distinction worth keeping.

Jinx set the eye apparatus on the desk. She adjusted it once, lining it up with the edge of a paper, then immediately moved it back.

“I need my things.”

“Okay.”

“And I'm coming back.”

“Okay.”

“Stop saying it like that.”

Vi nodded.

Jinx stood.

From downstairs came the heavy thump of something striking the bar.

Then Sevika's voice.

“Take your hand off it.”

* * *

The man with the bandaged hand had pulled the ledger toward him.

Sevika stood at the end of the bar. Two of the men from the booth had risen; another remained seated with his right hand beneath the table.

Kaze hadn't moved from the foot of the stairs.

“You took the depot,” the bandaged man told him. “Now you come here.”

“I came with her.”

“That supposed to make a difference?”

“To what I just said? Quite a fucking lot.”

Sevika planted her metal hand on the ledger.

“These accounts stay here.”

“Our people haven't been paid.”

“Then give me time to find out what's left.”

“So you can pay yours first?”

The man beneath the table drew his pistol.

Kaze saw the elbow move before the muzzle cleared the wood.

The pistol rose toward Sevika.

A black opening appeared beside the man's wrist. Kaze's hand came through it and closed over his knuckles.

The gun fired into the floor.

Kaze squeezed.

Bone broke inside the grip. The man screamed, and Kaze pulled his arm through the opening far enough to drag him face-first against the table's edge.

The other two went for their weapons.

They were still drawing when Kaze reached them.

He drove the first man backward with one hand around his throat. The wall stopped the body; the pressure of Kaze's palm did not. Something gave with a wet crack.

Kaze turned as the second man brought up a short shotgun.

His right forearm divided around the barrel. Black tissue wound over the weapon, trapped it against his arm and bent the muzzle away. The shot blasted bottles behind the bar. Kaze stepped into the man and struck him beneath the jaw.

His head snapped back too far.

By the time the broken shotgun hit the floor, Sevika had the bandaged man's face pressed against the ledger.

The fourth man in the booth sat perfectly still.

Both hands rested on the table.

Kaze looked at him.

The man pushed his untouched rifle away with two fingers.

“Good choice.”

On the stairs, Vi stood in front of Jinx.

Neither had needed to come down.

The whole ugly business had taken less time than the argument that preceded it.

Sevika hauled her captive upright. Blood from his nose ran over his mouth.

“Who told you to come?”

“Nobody.”

She twisted his arm.

“Nobody! We heard about the yard. We thought—”

“That I'd be alone.”

He said nothing.

Kaze walked toward him.

Sevika shifted between them.

“I'm using this one.”

Kaze looked at the man over her shoulder.

Then at the red smear across the ledger.

“He's leaking on your money.”

“I noticed.”

For a moment, I thought the lad might decide that being asked to wait was itself sufficient reason to make the request impossible.

Instead, he glanced toward Vi.

Jinx stood one step above her, looking over the bodies with a flat, exhausted attention that made the room feel colder.

Kaze stepped aside.

Sevika shoved the man toward his surviving companion.

“Take him. Tell the others I'm counting what's left. Anyone who comes here with a gun drawn gets counted separately.”

They left quickly.

The uninjured one came back only long enough to drag the man with the crushed wrist out from beneath the booth. Kaze let him. The injured man's abandoned pistol remained on the floor.

Jinx came down the stairs. She stopped beside the man whose neck Kaze had broken. His coat had fallen open. A cheap metal badge—one of Silco's warehouse marks—hung from a chain beneath it.

She stared at the badge.

Yesterday, that little scrap of metal had meant somebody would answer for what happened to him. Tonight, it lay in his blood.

Sevika saw where she was looking.

“Don't.”

Jinx looked up.

“Don't what?”

“Make every stupid thing they do about him.”

Jinx's face sharpened.

“You done?”

Sevika held her gaze. Then stepped away from the stairs.

Jinx retrieved a canvas bag from a cupboard behind the bar. Tools clinked inside it. She slung it over one shoulder, checked Zapper at her hip and started for the door.

Vi followed.

Kaze paused beside Sevika.

“You want those moved?”

She looked at the bodies.

“Outside.”

He took two by their coats.

“Your customer service is getting rough.”

“Get out.”

That, at least, sounded like an establishment recovering its standards.

* * *

Billy was still in the workshop yard when we returned.

Daniel sat on a crate beside him, working his way through the drink he had brought home. Arthur stood beneath the lamp with a cleaning cloth in one hand.

Jinx stepped out of Kaze's opening.

Then stopped.

Billy lowered his head.

“Well, now.”

Her eyes travelled over him. The shoulder ridges. The broad jaw. The enormous hand resting beside the doorway.

“You ate a bigger one?”

Daniel coughed into his drink.

Billy considered her.

“I see your sister has neglected to prepare you.”

“I said he got bigger,” Vi said.

“A tragically impoverished description.”

Jinx looked toward the banjo.

“Where's the other idiot?”

“Here,” Chris answered.

She nodded once, as if that settled some private concern.

“Dad.”

Billy's expression changed. The performance left him without taking the courtesy with it.

“This way.”

His mouth opened.

Jinx watched the head and jaw enlarge around the darkness within. She looked at Vi, who held her gaze and waited.

Then Jinx walked forward.

Kaze went with them.

Inside, Chris met us near the passage into MEAT CANYON. He had put the folded blanket back over the chair he'd found for Vi.

Jinx saw the chair.

Then the wall beyond it.

Vander stood behind the transparent flesh. Thin veins ran through the barrier. They shifted when his claws pressed against it.

Jinx stopped so abruptly that Vi nearly touched her back.

Vander's head lifted. His nostrils widened. The mechanism across his shoulders laboured beneath his breathing, a rough metallic accompaniment to the low sound gathering in his chest.

Kaze moved closer to the barrier.

Vander's eyes passed over him. Found Vi. Then Jinx.

The growl broke apart.

Jinx's hand closed around the strap of her bag.

“Hi.”

Such a small word for the distance it had to cross.

Vander leaned forward. His lips moved against teeth that made the effort look painful.

“Pow…”

Jinx looked down.

Vi reached her first, touching her elbow.

“Stay.”

“I am.”

But her feet had already begun to turn.

Vander struck the barrier with the heel of his hand. The living wall flexed. Jinx flinched; Kaze planted his palm against it, ready to cross if the next movement became an attack.

There wasn't one.

Vander lowered himself. He brought his face nearer theirs.

“Powder.”

Jinx made a sound that wasn't quite a laugh.

“Little late.”

Vi's grip tightened.

Jinx pulled free, but she didn't leave. She approached the wall.

Vander followed the movement with painful care. One claw dragged down the membrane, leaving a pale pressure line that slowly eased away.

Jinx lifted her hand. Held it short of his.

“I did it again.”

Vi closed her eyes.

“Jinx—”

“No. He should know.”

Vander watched her mouth.

Jinx tried to continue. I could see the effort in the little movement of her jaw, the way she pulled breath and found nothing she could make out of it.

At last she put her palm against the wall.

His hand covered the other side.

Vander did not tell her it was all right. He did not understand enough to offer forgiveness, and none of us had the right to put it in his mouth.

He stayed there.

For the moment, she let that be something.

Chris quietly drew the chair closer.

Jinx sat without looking at him.

Kaze stepped back. We gave them a little room, though not so much that he couldn't reach Vander if he needed to.

Beside me, Chris rubbed the back of his neck.

“Was going to put a couch here,” he murmured.

“Were you?”

“No.”

He watched the sisters.

“Might now.”

* * *

Morning found Chris outside with a cup of coffee and a body that still obeyed him faster than he expected.

He caught a falling screw before it struck the paving, looked at it, and put it back on the table. Arthur watched without offering the applause Chris had plainly considered requesting.

“Getting used to it?”

“Getting used to everybody acting like this isn't fucking incredible.”

Arthur lifted his cigar.

“You can be incredible after breakfast.”

Vi had left earlier, after Jinx asked Billy to let her out. The sisters walked as far as the end of the lane together. Then Jinx went back toward the Last Drop, and Vi took the climb toward Piltover. Vander remained in MEAT CANYON. Billy had returned the exterior to Chris only after both visitors were outside.

Kaze sat on the wall while Jesse brought out the remaining bread.

The knock at the gate belonged to Jayce.

He looked tired enough to have forgotten which part of the night had become morning. An Academy attendant waited behind him with a document case.

Chris lowered his cup.

“If you want a sample, start by saying please.”

“I want you to come with me.”

“Worse.”

Jayce didn't smile.

“Sky is dead.”

Chris set the cup down carefully.

Jayce looked past him at the equipment and the people around it.

“Viktor's experiment. The Hexcore.”

“What happened?”

“She tried to pull him away.”

For a moment, Chris looked at his own hands.

They had changed. He had wanted them to. Now there was someone he knew whose work had ended on a laboratory floor, and no clever comparison came to him.

“Viktor?”

“Alive. Changed.”

“Does he need—”

Jayce shook his head before Chris finished.

“I don't know what he needs. I know what happened cannot stay between a few people in a room.”

He explained the summons briefly. The Council was examining Hextech access, laboratory authority and the collapse of control around Silco's operations. Heimerdinger wanted the Hexcore isolated and destroyed under controlled conditions. Jayce wanted isolation, but opposed destruction until they understood what it would do to Viktor.

“And you want me because mine worked,” Chris said.

“I want you because you know the difference between a result and permission to give it to everyone.”

Chris looked at him for a long moment.

“Don't sell me as your proof.”

“Then come and say that.”

Kaze stood.

Jayce looked at him.

“They won't welcome you.”

“I gathered that.”

Chris picked up his coat.

“He's with me.”

* * *

The Council chamber held the morning differently from the streets beneath it.

Light came through high windows and travelled across polished floors before touching anybody's shoes. From the outer gallery, Chris could see roofs falling away toward Zaun, then the haze gathering where the city became work.

Kaze studied the doors, the guards and the expensive chair nobody had offered him.

“Whole lot of room for telling other people where to stand.”

Chris nudged him.

“I'm trying not to get thrown out yet.”

“Yet.”

Inside, Mel had brought forward a proposal to give Jayce a Council seat with direct responsibility for Hextech oversight. His inventions already helped carry the city's trade; decisions about their use were being made by people who still summoned him afterward to explain the consequences.

Heimerdinger supported giving him that responsibility. He said so before anyone mistook his caution for a desire to keep his pupil small.

“But responsibility does not make a dangerous experiment safe.”

Jayce stood beside the empty chair.

“I haven't said it does.”

The appointment passed. Mel watched Jayce sit, and Cassandra placed the waiting papers before him. He touched the top sheet as if it belonged to a different trade from anything his hands had learned.

Then they brought Chris in.

He answered what he knew. Singed had handled the biological interpretation. Walter and Jesse had contributed distinct practical work. Chris had set the project requirements, obtained the necessary sources and chosen to receive the treatment.

He did not give them a recipe. He did not offer anyone a vial.

“And the effects are permanent?” Cassandra asked.

“That's the result.”

“Safe for others?”

“Didn't say that.”

Heimerdinger leaned forward.

“Then you understand why this cannot be treated as a public programme.”

“I understand why you don't get to stick it in someone else because I didn't die.”

“Precisely.”

Chris glanced at Jayce.

The agreement had plainly surprised him more than an argument would have.

When the questions turned to Viktor, Chris stopped them.

“Different work. Different person. I haven't seen what happened to him. Don't use my body to explain it.”

Jayce nodded.

That was the useful part of Chris's visit. He had come expecting to defend his success and instead made it harder to turn that success into an excuse.

He and Kaze returned to the gallery while the Council argued over the laboratory order.

Heimerdinger's concern was specific. The Hexcore had continued acting beyond the controls its makers understood. A colleague had died in contact with it. Viktor's changes offered evidence of reach, not evidence of control.

Jayce agreed to seal the room, record every entry and stop further experiments. He would not authorize destroying the device before they had investigated its connection to Viktor's altered body.

“You are asking us to leave an active danger intact,” Heimerdinger said.

“I'm asking you not to make a second irreversible decision before we understand the first.”

“I have seen what this sort of promise costs.”

“Then help us measure this one.”

The old inventor looked at him with a sadness Chris could see even from the doorway.

“There are discoveries you do not get to finish safely.”

Jayce lowered his eyes.

When he raised them, he spoke about more than the chamber downstairs. Shimmer had spread while institutions passed problems between them. The undercity's losses reached the Council as delays and missing revenue. The same government now demanded speed from its inventors while keeping every decision dependent on authority that could defer it indefinitely.

Heimerdinger answered each point he could. He did not pretend Zaun had been well served. He also refused the bargain that every institutional failure should become permission for the next hazardous invention.

Neither man was foolish enough to be easily dismissed.

Jayce asked for Heimerdinger's removal from the Council anyway.

Chris went still.

Kaze looked through the doorway.

The vote took longer than Jayce had expected. Cassandra questioned what replaced the authority he wanted removed. Mel required him to accept the oversight work he had just demanded. The others weighed the threats to trade, their houses, and the power they would retain if the founder could no longer stop them.

Hands rose.

Heimerdinger watched Jayce's last.

It rose too.

The order that followed kept the Hexcore isolated and experiments suspended. Its destruction remained deferred for investigation. Heimerdinger's Council authority did not.

He stood without asking anyone to reconsider.

Jayce started after him.

The old inventor turned at the door.

“You must do the work now.”

“I will.”

Heimerdinger looked at the papers on Jayce's desk.

“I hope you understand how much of it there is.”

* * *

Chris found Jayce in the outer gallery afterward.

“You could've told me that was where this was going.”

Jayce leaned against the stone rail.

“I didn't come here certain.”

“You got certain pretty fucking fast.”

“Would you have let him decide what happened to your body?”

Chris shook his head.

“That isn't what you asked him.”

Jayce looked at him.

Chris nodded toward the chamber.

“You asked everyone else whether he got to keep his chair.”

For a while, the noise from the city below filled the space between them.

Kaze had stayed near the doors. Now he came over, looked at the rooftops and then at Jayce.

“Where's Viktor?”

“At the Academy. Resting.”

“Does he know what you just did?”

“Not yet.”

Kaze nodded once.

“Probably start there.”

Chris looked back at the chamber, then flexed his hand. He could still catch a falling screw before it reached the floor. Nothing that had happened here had taken that away from him.

It had become considerably harder to mistake that for understanding everything else.

* * *

Heimerdinger took the long way down.

By the time he reached the lower streets, the pale stone had given way to riveted iron and walls crowded close around the passage. He paused beside a public water point where a woman was arguing with a valve that would not shut.

He watched the water escape.

“The seating may have worn unevenly,” he said.

She looked at him.

“Then tell it to stop.”

He began to answer, stopped, and set his little case down.

“May I look?”

The woman stepped aside.

For the first time that day, someone gave him room without asking him to lead anything.

He knelt beside the pipe.

## Act VIII — Episode 3/3 — replacement edition

Chris returned from Piltover with the same hands and considerably less interest in showing them off.

Arthur noticed before Chris reached the workbench.

“Bad audience?”

Chris put his coat down.

“They voted Heimerdinger out.”

Daniel looked up from the chair he had dragged into the yard.

“The little guy?”

“That narrows it down beautifully.”

“The little guy with the fucking moustache.”

“Yes.”


Chris tried to answer in one sentence. Failed. Tried again.

“Jayce wanted responsibility. He got it.”

Kaze came through behind him.

“And a chair.”

“Expensive chair,” Chris said.

“Ugly as fuck.”

For a moment, Chris looked as if he might laugh. Instead he rubbed the bridge of his nose.

Arthur let the silence settle before asking the useful question.

“Anything change for us?”

“Nobody owns my work,” Chris said. “Said that in front of witnesses.”

“Good.”

“They didn't clap.”

“Ungrateful people.”

That got the laugh, small though it was.

Walter stood inside with Jesse beside the depot accounts. He listened to the news, then looked back at the names on the paper. Another authority had changed hands. Another man would now be asked for decisions he hadn't needed to make yesterday.

“We should speak to Jayce,” he said.

Chris pointed at him without looking.

“We just did. I need food.”

“It would be useful to establish—”

“Food, Walter.”

From within the bond, Billy made his own preference known when Chris yielded the exterior a few minutes later. Flesh turned through the opening, the broad new shoulders came out, and the yard once again had less room than its builders had intended.

Billy adjusted his hat.

“I should like to eat somewhere that does not smell of solvents.”

Daniel lifted his drink.

“It's a workshop.”

“A fact it has never once allowed me to forget.”

Kaze looked toward the gate.

“Where?”

“Waterfront,” Arthur said. “Passed a cookfire there yesterday.”

Billy considered him with immediate warmth.

“You are a man of uncommon value.”

“Remember that when the bill comes.”

* * *

The waterfront opened the city around us.

After Zaun's close lanes, the width of the water felt almost extravagant. Cargo cranes stood over long stone quays. Smoke drifted from kitchens beneath the warehouse roofs, mixing with salt, tar and fish guts. Higher behind us, Piltover's terraces caught the afternoon sun; beneath the loading platforms, the canal carried its darker business toward the harbour.

Arthur found the cookfire.

The woman tending it took one look at Billy and moved a basket farther from the edge of her table.

“Pay first.”

Billy laid coins beside her board.

She counted them.

Then looked at the basket again.

“How much?”

“Yes,” Chris said through the banjo.

Billy lowered one eye toward the instrument.

“We discussed your interruptions.”

“You discussed them. I was busy.”

The woman looked from the instrument to Kaze.

He shrugged.

“Comes with the fish.”

She decided payment was a more manageable subject and began setting food aside.

Daniel remained standing, tail curled clear of a passing cart, and watched a dockworker attempt to fit a long crate through a short doorway.

“Turn it.”

The man turned it the wrong way.

Daniel took a drink.

“Beautiful. Exactly that.”

Kaze laughed and sat on the edge of the quay.

For a while, nobody wanted him to decide who deserved saving, custody or forgiveness. He ate, watched the boats and flicked a fish bone toward the water.

Billy played.

His fingers moved with easy skill, keeping a little wandering melody alive while the woman at the fire discovered that he would pay for a second basket too.

Then the harbour tug sounded its horn.

Beyond the cranes, a dark-hulled ship was coming in.

The labourers noticed the banners before we could make out the people on deck. Work continued, but voices changed. Orders became shorter. A harbour official left his office fastening his coat, followed by two clerks carrying boards.

Daniel looked across the water.

“That's a cheerful-looking boat.”

Black and red hung above its rail.

Arthur followed the movement of the tug.

“Looks expensive to argue with.”

Kaze shaded his eyes.

On the ship's raised deck stood a broad-shouldered woman with a soldier's bearing and grey in her hair. She watched the quay as the vessel turned, giving the waiting officials no visible concern at all.

Kaze stopped chewing.

“That's Ambessa.”

Chris's voice came from the banjo.

“Oh, shit.”

Arthur looked at them.

“Friend of yours?”

“No,” Kaze said.

“Enemy?”

“Haven't met her.”

Arthur took a slow draw on his cigar.

“Try keeping it that way till I finish eating.”

* * *

The ship tied up at the next quay.

Its crew lowered the gangway while the harbour official tried to discover which form would make the arrival feel like something he controlled. Soldiers waited aboard with their weapons carried, not raised. Two watched the gangway; the others remained beside the passengers and the stacked cargo.

Ambessa descended first.

A large man followed a short distance behind her. He watched the hands of the officials, the open warehouse doors and the people along the rail.

Billy continued his tune.

Ambessa spoke to the harbour official. Whatever answer he gave sent one of the clerks hurrying toward the upper streets with a message for Councillor Medarda.

Kaze watched that part too.

“Family visit,” he said.

“With luggage that can stab you,” Daniel replied.

The trouble began above the cookfire.

On the adjoining loading platform, a foreman had been trying to clear a suspended cargo sling before the newly arrived ship took the berth. One worker shouted that the load was crooked. Another signalled the crane operator to stop.

The drum kept turning for half a revolution too long.

A worn fitting tore loose.

The sling dropped at one end, spilling a crate onto the platform and dragging the rest toward the edge.


The paving cracked under his rear foot. He lowered the crate onto its side instead of letting it roll through the cookfire.

His teeth closed around it; his paws dug into the quay.

The smaller crate broke apart beside the tables.

Kaze lifted his foot as splintered wood skidded past.

A heavy iron fitting flew toward the cook's board. His hand snapped out and caught it.

He looked at the crushed piece of fish beneath the settling debris.

“Motherfucker.”

Billy put his banjo aside and caught the last loose barrel before it could strike the woman. His tongue wound around it, drawing it neatly away from her legs.

She stood with a cooking knife in one hand, staring at the wreckage.

Billy looked from the barrel to the broken basket.

“I trust the remainder of my order was elsewhere.”

“Under that.”

His eyes moved toward the fallen crate.

For a moment, the foreman looked much less worried about the crane.


“You hurt?”

The man checked himself, then shook his head.

“Move away from the load.”

He moved.

Kaze stood and placed the iron fitting on the foreman's board hard enough to split it.

“Who's paying for lunch?”

The foreman began explaining the company.

“Didn't ask for the history.”

Coins appeared.

Kaze took them, counted them with exaggerated care, and turned back toward the cookfire.

Across the quay, Ambessa had stopped listening to the harbour official.

* * *

Mel arrived before the damaged cargo had been cleared.

She came down the steps with an attendant behind her, saw the ship, and slowed just enough for her mother to notice.

Ambessa opened her arms.

“Mel.”

Mel accepted the embrace. It was brief, and not quite as cold as the words that followed.

“You could have sent notice.”

“And deprived myself of seeing you surprised?”

“You've seen it now.”

Ambessa stepped back and studied her daughter. Fine clothes, composed face, a city moving around her name. There was pride in the look. Possession too, though Mel stood as if neither entitled her mother to take the next step for her.

“You have done well.”

“Why are you here?”

Ambessa glanced toward the soldiers unloading her baggage.

“Can a mother not visit?”

“A mother can. You bring other responsibilities.”

They walked away from the gangway toward a sheltered stretch of quay. Mel chose the direction, putting the harbour officials behind them and keeping the soldiers at a distance.

Ambessa allowed it.

“I hear your city has become very inventive.”

“It has been inventive for some time.”

“And less secure.”

Mel looked toward the damaged crane.

“An accident is not an invasion.”

“I wasn't speaking of the crane.”

The answer brought them to the subject Ambessa had crossed the water to reach: portable Hextech, failing local arrangements, and the risk that a family without the means to defend its position would discover too late that influence was not protection.

Mel listened. She did not offer workshop access, weapons or a seat at the Council table.

“We are reviewing security,” she said.

“Then it is fortunate I arrived.”

“For whom?”

Ambessa smiled a little.

“You used to ask that more quietly.”

“You used to hear it less often.”

Their attendant stopped several paces away. Rictus remained nearer the ship, watching while dockworkers moved the baggage past him.

Mel glanced toward the soldiers.

“Your escort stays with your vessel or your accommodation. No independent patrols.”

“You fear my people walking in your streets?”

“I expect armed guests to remember whose streets they are.”

Ambessa held her daughter's gaze for a moment.

Then nodded.

It was a concession. It was not the end of the subject.

* * *

Kaze was collecting his replacement food when Mel noticed him.

Her gaze moved from his face to the living black armour across his right shoulder. Then to Billy, whose banjo had resumed beneath a patient complaint about food with avoidable splinters in it.

Ambessa followed the glance.

“You know them.”

“I know who they are.”

“The large one caught that cargo.”


“And the other?”

Mel looked at Billy.

“You should ask him.”

Ambessa did.

She crossed the few yards between them without bringing her escort around her. Rictus followed at a distance that made his purpose plain while leaving the conversation hers.

Billy let the phrase end before looking up.

“Madam.”

“You handled the accident well.”

“I had considerable investment in the surrounding fish.”

Ambessa's eyes moved over the scutes along his shoulders, the massive hands and the instrument resting between them.


“He was under it.”

Ambessa nodded once. No grand explanation had been offered, and she did not pretend one was required.

Kaze came back with the food.

She turned toward him.

“You know my name.”

He looked at Mel, then back at her mother.

“Wasn't exactly whispering.”

“I haven't heard yours.”

“Kaze.”

“Whose service are you in?”

He stopped with the basket halfway between his hands and the table.

Then smiled.

“Mine.”

Ambessa considered him without returning the smile immediately.

“A costly arrangement.”

“Been worth it.”

Mel stood beside her mother now.

“They're not employed by the Council.”

“I didn't assume they were.”

“You asked who employed them.”

Ambessa looked at her daughter.

“And received an answer.”

Arthur drew on his cigar and watched the exchange with the patient suspicion of a man who had survived several kinds of employment.

Ambessa turned back to Kaze.

“Piltover has enemies. Capable people may find opportunity here.”

“Already found some.”

He set the basket down.

Chris spoke from the banjo.

“Very good fittings.”

Rictus's attention shifted to the instrument.

Billy smiled at him.

“A resident.”

“You carry companions?” Ambessa asked.

“Among other things.”

“Useful.”

“Often inconvenient,” Chris said.

Kaze laughed.

Ambessa watched the way they looked at one another. Nobody waited for Kaze to tell them whether they could speak. Arthur's hand remained nowhere near a salute.

“If you wish to discuss work,” she said, “my people can arrange it.”

Kaze picked up his food.

“If I want work, I'll ask what you're paying.”

“Good.”

“Didn't say yes.”

“I heard you.”

She let the conversation end there.

When she and Mel walked away, Daniel watched them for a moment before leaning toward Kaze.

“You think she charges extra for the family visit?”

“Probably bills by the threat.”

Arthur put out his cigar.

“That woman wasn't asking if you needed money.”

Kaze looked after her.

“I know.”

He was still smiling, but there was more attention behind it now.

* * *

On the steps above the quay, Mel stopped her mother before they reached the waiting carriage.

“You do not know what they are capable of.”

“I saw enough to ask.”

“Then ask me before you make promises involving my city.”

Ambessa turned.

“Your city?”

Mel held her gaze.

“Yes.”

For a moment, the harbour noise seemed very far beneath them.

“Then protect it,” Ambessa said.

“That is what I'm doing.”

Mel gave her the immediate facts: Silco dead, factions moving into the gaps, questions about weapons and biological research, a new councillor taking responsibility for Hextech, and Heimerdinger removed that day.

Ambessa's attention sharpened at the last two.

“Talis.”

“You have heard of him.”

“Everyone interested in the future has heard of him.”

“He is not your armourer.”

Ambessa looked toward the clean upper streets.

“You cannot decide what a man will want before he tells you.”

“Nor can you.”

They entered the carriage together.

Mel sat facing the door. Ambessa sat opposite her, close enough to be family, far enough to continue negotiating.

* * *

The message reached the Last Drop late that afternoon.

Sevika had reopened the front room to the people still coming for wages, deliveries and instructions. She had made no speech about inheriting Silco's position. The empty office above them made such speeches look particularly foolish.

Arthur was there with the depot ledger. He had brought it after Sevika sent a runner to ask who now held the accounts. Walter had retained the notes he needed; the original book lay open beneath Sevika's hand.

“Some of those wages went out of the strongbox,” Arthur said.

“Which ones?”

He showed her the entries Jesse had marked.

She read them twice before putting the book aside.

The Council courier waited near the door with a document case against his chest. His coat was clean. He looked determined to keep it that way.

Sevika signed his receipt.

“Anything else?”

“The hearing requires a representative able to answer for the accounts and operations.”

“Then tell them to bring good questions.”

He left.

Jinx came down the stairs before Sevika opened the second envelope.

“Who died now?”

“Nobody in that coat.”

“Shame.”

Jinx sat at the end of the bar. Arthur glanced at her, then left her the space to decide whether she wanted conversation.

Sevika read the notice.

Her expression changed.

Jinx saw it.

“What?”

“They want production records. Transport routes. The names behind payments to Enforcers.”

“They could ask their Enforcers.”

“Some of them are dead.”

“Could ask the ones spending it.”

Sevika set the page down.

Jinx took it.

She read quickly at first, then returned to the top.

“Criminal organizer,” she said.

Arthur looked toward her.

Jinx tapped the description beside Silco's name.

“That's all they need, apparently.”

“It isn't everything he was,” Sevika said. “It is something he was.”

Jinx looked up sharply.

Sevika didn't withdraw it.

The notice proposed restrictions on disputed Shimmer shipments while the Council considered who could lawfully speak for the affected operations. It also requested Jinx's transfer to Enforcer custody for questioning about her part in Silco's armed network and weapons work.

She read that paragraph twice.

“And there I am.”

“I haven't agreed to anything,” Sevika said.

“But they're asking.”

“Yes.”

Jinx folded the paper.

Arthur watched her hands.

“You want me to tell your sister?”

“No.”

“She'll hear about it.”

“Then you'll get a day off.”

He let that stand.

Jinx slipped the notice into her bag and went back upstairs.

She left the door open. From below, Sevika could hear her move the chair behind Silco's desk.

The chair scraped once.

Then the room was quiet.

* * *

Arthur returned to the workshop after the others.

Daniel had taken the chair Chris kept accusing him of stealing. Kaze sat on the wall with one foot against the stone.

“Council wants the accounts,” Arthur said. “And Jinx.”

Kaze looked up.

“She know?”

“Read it herself.”

“What'd she say?”

“Not much I'd mistake for surrender.”

Chris spoke through the banjo.

“Vi knows?”

“Not from me. I'll tell her when I see her.”

Kaze looked toward the upper city. There were too many roofs between here and the Last Drop to see it, and no amount of irritation gave him another person's intentions.

“Sevika taking her?”

“Didn't sound like it.”

Billy let the last note fade beneath his fingers.

“People are very fond of asking for deliveries they have no intention of collecting themselves.”

Arthur leaned against the doorframe.

“This one might get collected.”

Nobody answered immediately.

Then Chris said, “I still want the couch.”

Daniel looked toward the instrument.

“What couch?”

“For inside. By Vander.”

Billy glanced down.

“You complained the chair would encourage visitors.”

“They're visiting anyway.”

Kaze gave a short laugh.

“Look at you.”

“Don't make it a thing.”

“Too late.”

Billy began playing again.

At the Last Drop, Jinx sat in Silco's chair with the folded notice before her. She moved it away from his eye apparatus, leaving a clean strip of desk between them.

Then she read the names at the bottom.

This time, slowly.

The following fields describe only this repaired delivery edition. They do not promote story to accepted canon or overwrite the untouched s35 source package by implication.

| Chris's prior ignorance | He did not know before administration that Billy would evolve; his surprise remains in VII.3 |

| Ambessa | Arrives in VIII.3 with a bounded personal escort; meets Mel and the group; seeks Hextech/security influence; has no automatic authority over Piltover or the group |

| Caitlyn/Vi | Their relationship and earlier disagreements persist; no grief/commander phase from an attack that has not occurred; no VIII.3 attack admission or arrest confrontation |

| Arthur | Delivered depot ledger to Sevika; learned the new notice there and told the group; has not yet told Vi |

| Jinx's intentions | Private and not automatically known to Kaze, Vi, Caitlyn, Billy or Council |

## Forward plan restored — prospective only

**IX.1:** show the Council's argument over Zaun, Shimmer, Jinx, Hextech and Noxian involvement. Earn the actual attack weapon and usable political information through Jinx's choices and access. The notice alone is neither a completed weapon nor certainty about the eventual room's occupants. Give the main group a concrete independent objective that matters beyond keeping them off a roof. Do not assign knowledge from audience narration.

**IX.2:** earn Jinx's attack, Cassandra's fatal injury and Mel's instinctive protective response through the actual setup, access, weapon effect and room positions. Jayce's survival requires real protection or geometry. Do not place Kaze beside an announced launch and then suppress his abilities, add an invented cooldown, ignore a warning or use false helplessness. If actual delivery again establishes aware-and-able intervention, resolve it honestly and revise the setup before release rather than silently breaking the intended arc. Stay with impact and immediate aftermath.

**IX.3:** Caitlyn's loss, Vi's conflict, the government's injuries and Ambessa's available influence drive the security turn. Authority must be conferred and exercised on page; no automatic command title. X–XII retain their source-owned progression with prerequisites, character agency and real capability checks. No later event is marked completed by this repair.
