<!-- BEGIN EXACT SOURCE BYTES -->
The first barrier reached Zaun before the first order explaining it.

Four Enforcers rolled it down the descending road, iron wheels clattering over joints in the stone. Behind them came lamps, a second cart, and a young officer trying to unfold a map while he walked. At the bottom of the slope, a woman carrying laundry stopped beside a man with a sack of onions. Neither knew whether to move aside or turn back.

Above them, Piltover still had its evening light. Down here, the buildings caught what remained in narrow strips. Brass balconies gave way to iron galleries. Water dripped from a pipe overhead, striking the same dented roof with the patience of a creditor.

The officers put their barrier across the freight road.

Then they discovered the pedestrian opening led directly into somebody's locked shop.

Aye. The map had been very confident about that opening.

By the time they found another place for it, three carts had stopped on the slope. The third belonged to Arthur.

He stood beside its handles with a cigar between his teeth. Chris had strapped two cases of salvaged tools to the bed. A canvas sack of their clothes sat between those cases and a crate of bottles Daniel had bought from a quay merchant before the crossing restrictions tightened.

Most of the recovered machinery remained at the southern repair yard. Chris had chosen what he wanted with him. Arthur had chosen to get it off the quay while there was still a road available. Those choices had carried us into this particular inconvenience.

Daniel lifted the corner of the canvas.

“Something's leaking.”

Chris looked back.

“Your fucking purchase is leaking.”

“Could be somebody's trousers.”

Arthur took the cigar from his mouth.

“Don't make me think about that.”

Ahead, the onion seller tried to squeeze through the new pedestrian gap. An officer stopped him, pointed toward the inspection table and discovered that the table had not yet arrived.

Kaze watched from beside the cart.

His right shoulder and chest wore their living black armour. The rest of his torso was bare beneath the fading light, tattoos running into the dark tissue without a seam. His hands hung loose. He had been quiet since leaving the yard, which Chris had initially mistaken for patience.


There were alternatives visible from the waiting carts.

Arthur looked at his load.

“You carrying it?”

“Could.”

Chris shook his head.

“I am not taking my shit up somebody's stairs because their new government forgot people use roads.”

His nose moved.

Kaze glanced toward the inspection point.

“Let's hear what they want.”

He sounded almost interested.

That should have worried us more than it did.

* * *

Caitlyn was farther down, where the freight road met the first descending stair toward the Entresol.

She had stopped the front of the column while Vi argued with an officer over the route into a warehouse lane. Above them, the upper city's last sunlight ended at a stone arch. Beneath it, lamps burned behind stained glass, casting green and amber patches onto faces at the windows.

“There,” the officer said, touching his map.

“That entrance is bricked up.”

“The property records—”

“Come look.”

Vi took him six paces down the lane. The wall was exactly where she had said it would be, old mortar darkened by years of smoke. Somebody had painted a shop price across the bricks.

The officer looked at it.

Then at the map.

Caitlyn joined them.

“Where is the actual entrance?”

Vi pointed down a flight of steps beyond the arch.

“Shared courtyard. Three workshops and a family kitchen. You put your men through there with rifles up, they'll think you're raiding all four.”

“Then the rifles stay lowered until there's a threat.”

The officer nodded and went to change the approach.

For a moment, Caitlyn and Vi stood alone beside the useless entrance.

From the street came the sound of the column settling into place. Boots. Orders repeated by voices that had not heard the original. A child being called indoors.

Vi looked toward the stairs.

“You hear that?”

Caitlyn listened.

A shutter closed.

“People are frightened.”

“Of you.”

The words reached her with more force than Vi had put behind them.

Caitlyn adjusted the rifle strap against her shoulder.

“I can't make them trust us before we do anything.”

“You can give them room to leave.”

“There is a passage.”

Vi looked back toward the slope.

“There was supposed to be.”

Before Caitlyn could answer, a shot cracked above them.

Then another.

Both women turned.

* * *

The officer at the inspection point had started well enough.

He asked Arthur where the cart had come from. Arthur told him. He asked what it carried. Chris lifted one lid and showed him tools, folded clothing and a dismantled control assembly cushioned in cloth.

The man looked at the scorched metal.

“From the vessel?”

“What's left of it,” Chris said.

The officer looked at the scorched case, then at the clothes folded beside it. He seemed satisfied with what he could actually see.

He checked the cargo without touching the assembly.

Then his sergeant came over.

The sergeant was broad through the shoulders, with a scar beside one ear and the particular impatience of a man who had been given more work than people. He looked at Arthur's holsters. At Chris's revolvers.


“Weapons on the table before you proceed.”

Chris let the lid fall shut.

“No.”

“Temporary surrender for inspection.”

“Then temporarily inspect them where they are.”

Arthur rested one hand on the cart handle.

“You checked the load. Let us through.”

The younger officer shifted beside his sergeant.

“The harbour clarification excludes their private research—”

“I'm looking at guns.”

The sergeant's voice carried. People in the queue began watching instead of complaining.

He pointed at the two revolvers on Chris's belt.

“Those first.”

Chris looked at the hand, then at the man's face.

“You don't get to take those.”

“Name.”

“Chris.”

“Company or affiliation.”

Chris's mouth tilted.

“The Seven Sins.”

Daniel leaned on the cart.

“Spelled how it sounds.”

The younger officer had a pencil out. He wrote the name without knowing what it was going to cost him to remember it.

The sergeant stepped around the inspection table.

“Hands away from the weapons.”

Chris's hands were already away from them.

Kaze moved into the space between the two men.

“He answered you.”

“Step aside.”

“You checked our shit. Open it.”

The sergeant looked past him , perhaps expecting the largest man present to understand what the smaller one did not.


“Let us go,” he said.

The sergeant reached for Kaze's upper arm.

Kaze caught his wrist before the fingers closed.

For an instant, they stood almost politely together.

“Don't do that again.”

Kaze released him.

The sergeant's face changed. People had seen the contact. He knew they had seen it.

He drew his pistol and brought it against Kaze's bare left side.

“Against the barrier. Now.”

Kaze looked down at the weapon.

Then back at him.

“Nigga.”

There was amusement in it. Something worse than anger, for the man who needed obedience.

The sergeant shoved with the pistol.

Kaze's right hand closed around the gun and the hand holding it.

The barrel folded. Bone broke beneath the metal. The sergeant made a strangled sound and tried to pull away, but Kaze stepped closer, turning the trapped wrist until the elbow followed it in a direction an elbow had no business going.

An Enforcer beside the barrier fired.

The bullet struck Kaze's blackened shoulder and flattened against it.

He turned toward the shot.

“Oh, you too?”

The rifleman worked the action.

Kaze reached him before he could finish. His palm struck beneath the man's breastplate, driving metal into the chest and the body backward into the inspection table. Wood collapsed. The man hit the ground with blood rising in his mouth and never completed another breath.

At the far end of the barrier, a second rifle came up toward Chris.

CHOW MAIN cleared its holster.

Chris fired once.

The officer dropped across the cart wheel behind him, the rifle falling from his hands.

Those were the shots Caitlyn heard.

The rest happened beneath the noise they had already made.

* * *

Arthur let go of the cart.

The barrier had two sections, with the inspection point between them. Four officers stood beyond the farther section. Two were trying to see what had happened. A third was bringing his rifle to bear on Kaze's back.

Arthur drew one heavy hand cannon.

The shot punched through the man's chest and struck sparks from the iron behind him. He fell against the barrier. Arthur turned toward the next muzzle without hurrying.

That officer lowered it.

“Keep it there,” Arthur said.


He caught the rifle, wrenched it from the man's hands and struck him once with an open palm. The officer left his feet and hit the side of a freight wagon. His helmet broke against the wood. He slid down, breathing in ugly, uneven pulls.


The man saw the dog coming and changed his mind too late.

He went down hard. Jaws closed around the forearm he had raised, holding him against the road while his free hand clawed at the stones.


“Hold.”

The dog held.

No sermon. No committee. The lad had seen a weapon going toward his family and dealt with it as a dog of his size could.

Daniel had gone over the side of the cart.

His bare foot caught an iron bracket beneath a balcony. He swung around it, landed behind the barrier and drove his heel into the wheel-lock. The pin snapped. The section shifted sideways as an officer tried to brace it.

Ice ran over the wheel and the stones beneath it.

The man slipped. The barrier lurched into a little handcart waiting beside the onion seller.

The woman behind that cart cried out as its handle struck her wrist.

Daniel looked toward the sound.

“Ah, shit.”

She crouched, holding her hand. Her bottles rolled into the gutter.

He saw her. Then he saw the officer reaching for him, turned, and drove an elbow into the man's jaw.

The officer fell.

Daniel went on clearing the route.

Behind us, people were running uphill. Others flattened themselves against shutters, trapped between the waiting freight and the first gap they could reach. The onion seller abandoned his sack. Onions rolled through the rifleman's blood, acquiring an unfortunate new distinction in the produce trade.

Kaze still had the sergeant's wrist.

The man was on his knees now.

“Stop,” he gasped.

Kaze crouched in front of him.

“Remember when I said that?”

The sergeant looked toward his dead rifleman.

Then back.

“We were ordered—”

“I heard you.”

Kaze pressed the ruined hand flat against the broken tabletop and leaned on it.

The scream carried down the slope.


He moved to stand between those rifles and Kaze.

* * *

The younger inspection officer had escaped down the side stair.

He reached Caitlyn with the pencil still clenched in one hand.

“The checkpoint. They're killing—”

“Who?”

He struggled for breath.

“Kaze. The people from the harbour. The Seven Sins.”

Vi was already moving.

Caitlyn caught the officer long enough to make him look at her.

“How many?”

“Six. One's—there's a dog.”

“Our officers?”

“I don't know.”

She released him.

“Get the physicians from the rear. Tell the next unit to hold at the arch until I give an order.”

Then she followed Vi up the stairs.

On the road above them, the approaching detachment had stopped at the turn.

He spread his men behind the stone parapet.

“Weapons down!”

Arthur looked toward him.

“That ain't worked yet.”

Chris crouched beside the cart and opened one of the cases.

Kaze rose, leaving the sergeant folded over his ruined arm.

“More?”

His eyes had brightened.

Chris looked up.

“I want this stuff somewhere they can't fucking stand on it.”

Kaze indicated the dark opening forming beside his own hand.

“I can take it back.”

Chris considered the way we had come. The quay. The harbour order. The wounded they had just finished carrying off the vessel.

Then he shut the case.

“No. Billy.”

It was his choice to give up the outside. He made it angrily, which did not make it less a choice.

He holstered his revolver and stood clear of the cart.

His jaw opened.

I have seen that change enough times to know when to move my feet. Understanding the procedure has done very little for my appetite.

The human mouth pulled wide around folding flesh. Armour and limbs went inward as the larger body forced its way through, shoulders rising, belly settling, wet hide closing over the last vanishing shape of Chris.

Billy planted his feet on the road.

The officers behind the parapet watched ten feet and more of swamp brute straighten beneath a hat that had somehow preserved its dignity through the whole foul transaction.

His broad snout turned toward the fallen men.

Then toward the rifles.

“What a troublesome little reception.”

From the banjo against his belly, Chris said, “Get the cases in.”

Billy examined the cart.

“You have brought refreshments.”

“Tools.”

Daniel lifted the leaking crate.

“Some refreshments.”

Billy opened his mouth.

Arthur pushed the cart close enough to unload. Cases, canvas sack and crate passed through the immense jaws, each swallowed and routed under Billy's own control. Daniel protested when Billy took the bottles with the crate; the banjo promised, in Chris's voice, to put them somewhere Daniel would not find them immediately.

Billy let the empty cart stand.

An officer above us shouted for the last time.

“Get away from the injured!”

Billy glanced at the sergeant.

“My dear fellow, he is injured wherever I stand.”

The detachment's commander lifted his arm.

Rifles settled over the parapet.

Billy's expression became pleasantly attentive.

The road darkened beneath his feet.

* * *

Water reached the barrier first.

It shone between the paving stones without washing soil out from beneath them. Then the gaps widened into murky channels. Roots emerged beside the inspection point, curved through iron rails and lifted curtains of dripping moss. Fog moved low along the slope.

The shops remained where they were. Their steps rose above black water. The empty cart stood on a strip of firm ground Billy had left beneath its wheels.

Beyond it, the detachment's boots broke through the road.

One man dropped to his waist.

Another plunged beneath the surface before he understood what had happened.

The commander caught the parapet. His fingers scraped stone as his feet found nothing below them. Two of his officers clung to him, pulling all three toward the water.

Billy plucked a string.

The note floated through the fog.

“There we are.”

He began a slow, welcoming tune.

The young officer who had run for Caitlyn returned to the upper landing and stopped. He could see his commander hanging from the parapet. Could hear a man coughing somewhere in the channel beneath it.

“Help them!”

Billy smiled toward him.

“They are welcome to ask.”

Kaze looked over the water.


“That solves the road.”

He looked as if he might speak.

Kaze touched his shoulder as he passed.

“They pointed them at Chris.”


He stood beside him.

At the stair, Caitlyn reached the landing with Vi.

Her eyes moved across the scene: broken inspection table, bodies, a blood-covered sergeant, Billy playing beside a road that had become a swamp.

Vi found Kaze first.

“What the fuck happened?”

He spread his empty hands.

“Inspection.”

Caitlyn raised her rifle.

The barrel found his chest.

Kaze looked directly at her.

His smile stayed.

* * *

I would like to tell you somebody chose the sensible sentence at that moment.

What happened was Caitlyn saw the dead man beneath the ruined table, and Kaze saw her see him, and neither was thinking about a sentence anybody else could live comfortably inside.

“Put it down,” he said.

“Move away from my officers.”

“Your officers?”

“Yes.”

He glanced toward the sergeant.

“You train them to grab people?”

“You killed them.”

“I did.”

Vi stepped forward until she stood at the edge of the altered road.

“Kaze.”

He looked at her.

“Don't.”

She stopped. Not because he had made her afraid to speak. Because she heard what he was asking her not to pretend this was.

Caitlyn's finger rested beside the trigger.

Billy let the tune continue beneath her voice.

“Bring them out.”

“You have such confidence in the imperative.”

A man surfaced beside the parapet, clawing at the stone. He coughed water and tried to hook one elbow over the edge.

Caitlyn shifted toward him.

Billy watched.

Vi caught Caitlyn's arm before she stepped off the landing.

“Wait.”

Billy struck a brighter chord.

“Your companion is paying attention.”

Caitlyn's jaw tightened.

“They are trying to keep another attack from happening.”

“Then tell them to stop pointing guns at my people,” Kaze said.

“They were doing their jobs.”

“That supposed to matter to me?”

She stared at him.

There it was, with no better answer hiding behind it. A job was a reason the man had come to the barrier. Kaze had understood the reason. He simply had not given it authority over him.

The sergeant moaned.

Caitlyn looked .

“You let him do this?”


“I backed him.”

“That man isn't a criminal.”

“I know.”

He said it without pride. Without looking away, either.

Caitlyn's expression changed as something she had thought she understood about him ceased to be enough.

Vi looked at the injured officers, then at the people trapped between carts uphill.

The woman whose wrist Daniel had struck was still crouched beside her bottles.

“Billy,” Vi said. “Give us a way to get the hurt people out.”

He looked down at her.

“And what will you do with it?”

“Carry them.”

“A refreshingly literal answer.”

He stopped playing.

Chris spoke from the banjo.

“Tell them our stuff stays with us.”

Caitlyn kept her eyes on Kaze.

“I'm not bargaining over evidence in a murder.”

“Then don't,” Chris said. “But you aren't getting my cases.”

The commander at the parapet lost one handhold.

Caitlyn looked toward him.

Vi waited beside her.

For several seconds, the sounds of the street filled the place where an order ought to have been.

Then Caitlyn lowered her rifle.

“The detachment withdraws to the arch. The wounded come with us. No further attempt to disarm you here.”

She looked at Billy.

“Bring them out.”

“That last part needs work.”

But he turned toward the parapet.

Mud rose beneath the coughing man. A bank formed beside him, roots thick enough to grip. The others found shallower footing as the channel changed. They struggled toward the openings Billy left accessible, hauling one another out with all the poor grace of people who had recently discovered their own weight.

Billy watched each emerge.

He did not hide his pleasure.

* * *

Physicians came by the side stair with two folding stretchers.

Billy let them cross on solid ground. He permitted the injured officers to remain above the water. That permission was his to give, and every man who had gone under understood it in a way an explanation would never have achieved.

The officer cradled it against his chest. Teeth had opened the sleeve and the flesh beneath, though the bone still held its shape.


He lifted the one blocking the uphill gap and set it against the wall. People began passing through the space he made. The onion seller went first, abandoning what he could not gather quickly.

The woman with the injured wrist tried to retrieve a bottle with her good hand.

Daniel stood nearby.

She looked at him.

“You did that.”

His expression lost its grin.

“Didn't mean to catch you.”

“You weren't looking.”

He glanced toward the barrier he had moved.

“Needed it out the way.”

She held his gaze until he looked elsewhere.

A physician came over and asked to see her hand. Daniel moved aside. He stayed long enough to hear that she needed it bound, then went back to the gang.

Vi had heard the exchange.

She watched him pass before turning to help lift an Enforcer onto a stretcher.

Caitlyn knelt beside the sergeant.

His eyes were fixed on Kaze across the road.

“Look at me,” she said.

He tried.

“Tell the physician where it hurts.”

The man made a sound that might have been a laugh if his teeth had not been chattering.

The physician took his good arm and began asking smaller questions.

There were three dead officers. They covered the faces before carrying them. One coat was too short; a boot remained visible beneath its edge.

Caitlyn watched that boot go past.

When she stood, she went straight to Kaze.

Vi moved with her.

“This will be reported.”

Kaze nodded.

“Spell my name right.”

“You think this is funny?”

“Some of it.”

She looked toward the broken table.

He followed her gaze.

“You want me to tell you I'm sorry?”

“I want you to understand what you've done.”

He turned back to her.

For once, the smile went away.

“I understand.”

That was worse than ignorance. Caitlyn knew it. Vi knew it. Even the little lad holding the bloodied stretcher cloth behind them seemed to know it.

Caitlyn stepped back.

“The rest of the operation continues.”

“Then continue.”

“And if I find Jinx—”

“That ain't what happened here.”

His voice sharpened.

“Don't use her to make it something else.”

Vi looked between them.

Caitlyn held Kaze's gaze for another moment, then turned toward her officers.

She sent the surviving detachment back uphill with the bodies. She ordered the freight barrier removed from the pedestrian route and the local cordon shifted to the warehouse approach below the arch.

When one man asked whether he should leave a unit watching the gang, she looked at the water still moving beside Billy's feet.

“From the upper landing. Do not engage.”

Arthur watched them go.

“There's the second inspection.”

Chris answered from the banjo.

“One star. Nobody wash their hands.”

Kaze laughed.

Across the lane, the woman with the bound wrist heard him.

She turned away.

* * *

Vi caught Caitlyn beneath the arch.

The column waited beyond them. Enforcers who had not seen the fight watched the stretchers pass and tried to read the answers in their commander's face.

“Your first order was cargo,” Vi said.

“I know.”

“He made it their guns.”

“I will investigate what he did.”

“After.”

Caitlyn turned on her.

“Yes, Vi. After. He's on that stretcher. Three men are dead. I can't conduct an interview in the middle of Billy's fucking swamp.”

Vi took the anger. She had expected it.

“Then change what the next sergeant thinks he's allowed to do.”

“And what do you think changes Kaze?”

That stopped her.

From above came one thin banjo note.

Caitlyn looked toward it.

“He didn't lose control.”

Vi's shoulders lowered.

“No.”

“He wanted to keep going.”

“Yes.”

Caitlyn's hand tightened on the rifle strap.

“Then don't ask me to call this a misunderstanding.”

Vi stepped closer.

“I'm asking you to stop your people making it easier for him.”

For a while, neither spoke.

Then Caitlyn called the officer carrying the map.

She gave him the revised instruction herself. Cargo inspections required a cargo purpose. Officers were to request cooperation first. Any attempt to seize personal weapons from the gang required her direct authorization. Civilians were to have an actual usable passage, checked on foot before barriers went up.

He repeated it back.

She made him repeat the part about the passage again.

Then she sent him to the other units.

Vi watched him go.

Caitlyn turned toward the warehouse lane.

“We still search the stores tied to the weapons accounts.”

“I know.”

“And we still find her.”

Vi looked down the stairs, where the lamps had begun to brighten as the last daylight left the roofs.

“I know.”

This time, she followed Caitlyn without reaching for her hand.

* * *

Walter White reached the freight turn after the stretchers had gone.

He came uphill with Jesse and a porter pushing a narrow trolley. Empty reaction vessels stood in a padded rack beneath a canvas cover. At the cordon below, an officer had stopped them, read their delivery note and told them the receiving lane would remain closed while a weapons search took place.

Walter had asked how long.

The man had pointed toward the occupied street.

So Walter had come to look.

He found the broken barrier, the marks beneath the ruined inspection table, and Billy sitting beside a channel that had not existed on his earlier journey through the district.

Daniel was drinking from a bottle Chris had eventually allowed back outside. Arthur stood near the cart, which no longer carried anything worth pushing.

Kaze sat on the inspection table's one surviving corner.

Walter stopped.

His black hat shaded his eyes. He took it off before looking carefully at the blood.

“What happened?”

Chris's voice came from Billy's banjo.

“They inspected us.”

Jesse looked toward the water.

“Jesus.”

Billy inclined his head.

“He has not joined us.”

Walter looked down the route Caitlyn's people had taken.

“They've closed the delivery lane.”

Arthur put his cigar back between his teeth.

“They've been closing things.”

“That equipment needs to reach the receiving room intact.”

Kaze glanced toward the covered trolley.

“Then take it.”

“The officers won't allow it through.”

“Sounds like your problem.”

Walter put his hat back on.

I knew that little settling of the shoulders by then. The reasonable man arranging himself around an answer he had already decided someone else ought to give.

“It is also your friend's problem. There are supplies Chris still needs. Replacement glass. Solvents. Equipment that doesn't arrive because somebody has decided every chemical container belongs to a weapons operation.”

“I can get my own stuff,” Chris said.

Walter looked at the banjo.

“Of course you can. The question is whether you want to do it every time.”

Jesse shifted beside the porter.

“We just need the street, man.”

Billy looked at the trolley, then down the slope.

The fog had thinned enough to show where the upper strip of bayou ended. Beyond it lay the side landing, the shared steps and the courtyard Vi had identified. There was room to take a narrow trolley down that way if its handlers did not insist on using the blocked freight approach.

“You may bring your little carriage beside me,” Billy said.

Walter followed his gaze.

“And then?”

“Then you discover whether your wheels fit.”

Kaze smiled at that.

Walter did not.

He came closer anyway.

Billy let the porter onto firm ground. The man watched every step, lifting his feet higher than necessary. Jesse took the front of the trolley when they reached the narrowest part.

Walter remained beside Kaze.

“If this continues, movement becomes the business.”

“You asking for a job?”

“I'm saying whoever controls a usable route controls what the restrictions can actually stop.”

Kaze looked toward Billy.

Billy heard him perfectly well.

The banjo gave a soft answering chord.

Walter turned toward the trolley and followed it down.

He had got his glassware moving. He had also seen something worth returning to. The second fact showed in the way he looked back before the courtyard hid him.

* * *

Above us, an Enforcer watched from the upper landing with a notebook open against the parapet.

Billy knew where the man stood. He could feel the positions of people within the ground he had changed; the notebook's words remained the officer's own concern.

Kaze looked up.

The officer stiffened.

“You got the name?” Kaze called.

The man did not answer.

“Ask the little one with the pencil.”

Arthur gave a low chuckle.

Billy began another tune.

Beside the closed shop, two porters argued quietly about what they had seen. One said the Enforcers had started it. The other said starting an argument did not explain three bodies.

The onion seller, farther uphill, was telling a woman that the water had swallowed soldiers and left him standing.

Nobody had the whole of it.

Nobody needed the whole of it to carry something away.

From below the arch came the crash of a warehouse door giving beneath an Enforcer ram. A shout followed. Then another, farther inside.

Kaze turned toward the noise.


“Where you going?”

Kaze stood.

“Have a look.”

He stepped toward the side stair.

Arthur stayed beside Billy, rolling his shoulders as he looked down the new channel. Daniel settled onto the empty cart with his bottle.

The gang had room to go their separate ways now.

Above the turning road, the notebook shut. Its owner sent a runner uphill with three dead names, a description of impossible water, and the name Chris had given at the table.

The runner could only go as fast as his legs would carry him.

Billy's music followed him to the next bend.

<!-- END EXACT SOURCE BYTES -->

## x2

ID: `S37_X_2`. Original file: `KAZE_Act_X_Episode_2.md`. UTF-8 bytes: 29512. SHA256: `84e584331b1595971a7e3b02ef6a160b0a3b32a1547c02c1109222acf44f4c16`.

Complete selected delivered-provisional X.2 text; not accepted story.

<!-- BEGIN EXACT SOURCE BYTES -->
The warehouse door lay across two steps, its hinges still attached to the frame it had torn out of the wall.

Kaze stepped over it.

Inside, the Enforcers had found what they came for. Rifle components filled three open crates beneath the loading gallery. Two more held ammunition wrapped in grease paper. A clerk knelt beside them, comparing the markings with a page from Caitlyn's file while an officer kept the warehouse workers against the opposite wall.

There were nine of them. One had a bandage over his eye. Another kept asking whether somebody could fetch his coat from upstairs.

Nobody had answered him yet.


Kaze looked around.

“Busy.”

The officer by the workers turned.

He recognized Kaze. I saw his hand tighten around the rifle before he remembered the instruction that had reached him from the arch.

“This property is under search.”

“I can see that.”

“You need to stay outside.”

Kaze walked toward a little office beneath the gallery.

The officer watched him go. Then called for his commander.

The office door stood open. Inside, somebody had pulled papers from a drawer and left them scattered across the desk. An iron cashbox sat beside a half-eaten supper. Its lock had been forced during the search.

Kaze lifted the lid.

Coins. Folded notes. Two little cloth purses with names written on their ties.

He picked one up.

From the wall behind him, the man with the bandaged eye said, “That's the wages.”

Kaze looked back.

“Yours?”

“Some of it.”

He opened the purse, looked inside and emptied it into the box.

“Where'd the rest come from?”

“Ask the owner.”

“Where's he?”

The man indicated a shuttered window above the gallery.

Kaze looked up.

Something shifted behind the shutter.

He smiled.

“There you are.”

* * *

Caitlyn reached the office as he was lifting the cashbox from the desk.

She stopped in the doorway. Vi stood behind her, one hand still dusty from carrying a stretcher.

“Put that down.”

Kaze turned the box in his hands.

“Evidence?”

“Yes.”

“Expensive evidence.”

“It stays here.”

Above them, a shutter banged open.

A man in a long coat climbed onto the gallery rail. He had waited until the officers were looking at Kaze, which was a sensible use of an otherwise dreadful situation.

He jumped toward the loading platform outside.


The fabric tore. The man swung against the gallery supports with a howl, boots scrabbling for a surface they could not reach.


The man stopped trying to run.

Kaze came out of the office carrying the cashbox.

“That the owner?”

The workers said nothing.

Caitlyn indicated the crates.

“Where were those going?”

“I rent storage.”

“The entries carry your signature.”

“People sign things.”

Vi exhaled through her nose.

The man looked toward Kaze. Toward the box in his hands.

“That's mine.”

Kaze glanced at the workers.

“They think some of it's theirs.”

“They get paid when the accounts clear.”

“Do they?”

Kaze held the box against his hip and stepped closer.


“You can't just—”

“I can.”

Caitlyn moved between them before the man could finish whatever sentence he had hoped would make the world less unfair to him.

“He comes with us.”

Kaze looked at her.

Then at the man.

“You better have useful answers.”

He turned toward the door.

“The money,” Caitlyn said.

He kept walking.

Vi followed him outside.

“Some of that belongs to the workers.”

“They should've got paid earlier.”

“Kaze.”

He looked at her. The grin had not gone away; it had simply become less welcoming.

“You want it, come get it.”

She stopped on the broken threshold.

Behind her, Caitlyn ordered the theft entered beside the inventory. The clerk wrote while the man with the bandaged eye watched Kaze leave with the box.

There would be a version of tonight in which we had freed the road for ordinary people.

That man was going to have considerable difficulty telling it.

* * *

At the Last Drop, Sevika had three people waiting by the bar and a fourth refusing to wait anywhere.

The fourth was a woman carrying an empty medicine crate. She set it on the counter hard enough to make the glasses jump.

“Your men took the rest.”

Sevika looked at the crate.

“Which men?”

“Blue around their arms. Down by the service bridge.”

“Not mine.”

“They said the new road belongs to Jinx.”

Upstairs, a chair scraped.

Sevika heard it.

The woman pulled a folded scrap from her pocket. It had been torn from wrapping paper. Someone had drawn a crooked blue face above three words.

PASSAGE. PROTECTION. CONTRIBUTION.

Below them, in a different hand, somebody had written Jinx's name.

Sevika flattened it against the bar.

“How many?”

“Four where I saw them. More behind the shutters. They've got a wagon across the bridge.”

“Did you pay?”

The woman looked at her empty crate.

Sevika took her coat from the back of a chair.

Jinx appeared at the top of the stairs.

She had tied one braid back badly. The other hung loose beside her face. There was a dark smear across her fingers from the launcher mechanism she had been taking apart upstairs.

“What's mine now?”

Sevika held up the paper.

Jinx came down.

She looked at the face first.

“That's awful.”

Then she read the words.

Her expression changed.

“Who did this?”

“That's what we're finding out.”

The woman looked from one of them to the other.

“You didn't send them?”

Jinx's eyes lifted.

“Do I look like I sent them?”

The woman had no safe answer to that.

Sevika picked up the empty crate and gave it back.

“Show us where.”

Jinx took the paper from her.

She folded it carefully enough to crease the painted face through both eyes.

* * *

By the time we returned to Billy, his water had acquired an audience.

They kept a respectful distance. A dock porter stood beside a woman with two baskets. Three young adults occupied the stairs below the closed shop, looking from Billy to the Enforcer watching on the upper landing. One had tied a strip of blue cloth around her wrist.

Daniel was still on the empty cart. Arthur had moved to the wall, where he could see both approaches without turning his head.

Walter and Jesse had come back from the courtyard.

Their trolley was empty now.

“Money,” Chris said as Kaze approached.

“Look at you. Eyes still work.”

Billy examined the cashbox.

“A collection?”

“Something like that.”

Kaze set it on the cart beside Daniel.

Walter's eyes went to the box. Then to the road.

“There is a practical arrangement available here.”

Arthur removed his cigar.

“He been saying that since he got back.”

“Because the situation has not become less practical.”

Walter came to stand where Billy could see him clearly.

“A single receiving point. People bring their goods to the upper turn. We verify the load, arrange the onward movement and keep the route from clogging.”

Billy's barbels moved against his jaw.

“We?”

“I can provide the organization.”

“You have brought a trolley.”

Jesse looked down.

For a moment, I feared the lad was going to laugh in front of him.

Walter continued.

“What you have is access. Access can be wasted. It can also become something people depend on.”

Chris answered from the banjo.

“Don't start giving my house a fucking job.”

“I'm discussing the street.”

“You're discussing Billy sitting here all day while you tell people you're in charge.”

Walter paused.

The girl on the stairs had come closer. Blue cloth shone at her wrist beneath the shop lamp.

“Does she know?” she asked.

Billy looked down.

“You will need to be more specific.”

“Jinx. About what you did.”

Kaze turned toward her.

“What'd we do?”

“Kept them from shutting the way.”

He glanced at the empty cart.

“They tried to take our shit.”

“That's what they're doing to everyone.”

“Then everyone should stop letting them.”

She seemed to like that answer more than he had intended her to.

Arthur noticed.

“Careful,” he murmured.

The girl looked at Billy again.

“The men down the stairs said they work for her. Said you were keeping the upper road open and they'd keep the bridge.”

Billy stopped touching the strings.

The silence made her take a step back.

“Did they?”

She nodded.

Kaze looked toward the side stair.

Then at Walter.

“There. Somebody already got your job.”

Walter's face tightened.

Jesse took hold of the trolley.

“We should go check our stuff.”

This time, Walter agreed.

* * *

The service bridge crossed a narrow industrial cut beneath the freight road.

From above, it had looked like a short way into the courtyard. Down here, its width was less reassuring. A drain ran beneath the grating. Steam pipes hugged the wall on one side, and the railing on the other had been patched with two kinds of wire. Far below, machinery beat through the dark in a rhythm you could feel against your teeth.

A wagon stood across the far end.

Three people waited in front of it. Four others held the stair behind us. They had chosen the place well: somebody carrying a load could neither turn easily nor pass without coming close.

Blue cloth appeared on arms, belts and one man's throat. It did not make them identical. One wore a dock coat. Another had the polished shoes of a man who preferred other people to carry things for him.

The polished shoes stood beside the wagon.

Kaze came down first. Arthur remained above with Daniel, watching the upper approach. Billy stayed at the turn, where the road was still his and the Enforcer was still watching.

Walter and Jesse brought the empty trolley behind us.

The man by the wagon raised one hand.

“Passage contribution.”

Kaze stopped.

“To what?”

“Protection.”

“From who?”

The man indicated the upper city with his chin.

“You seen the uniforms.”

“You seen the bodies?”

That changed his attention.

He looked properly at Kaze. At the black right arm.

Recognition arrived in fragments.

“You're with the big one.”

“The big one's with us.”

“Good. Then we're on the same side.”

Kaze smiled.

“You sure?”

The man took a folded sheet from his coat. Another blue face occupied its top corner, better drawn than the one the medicine woman had carried.

“Jinx wants the routes secured. We collect what's needed.”

“She tell you that?”

“We have people.”

“That wasn't what I asked.”

Behind the wagon, Jesse could see a crate with the supplier's mark he had just helped carry into the courtyard.

He went still.

“Mr. White.”

Walter followed his gaze.

The crate held sealed bottles. Their receiving-room porter stood beside it with his hands bound behind his back.

Walter stepped past Kaze.

“Those have been paid for.”

The man looked at him.

“Then you've got enough to pay for them getting there.”

“They were already there.”

“And now they're here.”

Jesse moved toward the bound porter.

One of the men at the stair put a hand against his chest.

“Stay where you are.”

Jesse knocked the hand aside.

The man drew a knife.


The knife stopped halfway between the two men.

Kaze looked at the fellow beside the wagon.

“You charging for Billy?”

“We said he was holding—”

“You charging for him?”

The man glanced toward the stairs, where Arthur's hat had appeared above the rail.

His polished shoes shifted.

“There's a misunderstanding.”

“No.”

Kaze came closer.

“You're doing fine. Keep explaining.”

* * *

Jinx reached the lower landing from the opposite alley.

Sevika came behind her with the medicine woman. They had taken the route she knew from her deliveries, through a covered passage smelling of hot metal and old washwater. It brought them below the wagon instead of through the men at the upper stair.

Jinx heard her name before she saw the faces.

Then she heard Kaze.

“I want to know what you promised.”

She climbed the last three steps.

The man beside the wagon was looking away from her, sweating beneath his blue neckcloth.

“Safe passage.”

“With somebody else's name.”

“People needed something to believe in.”

Jinx stopped behind him.

“And you needed a till.”

He turned.

For a moment, no one moved.

Sevika took in the bound porter, the bottles, the medicine crate tucked beneath the wagon. Her metal hand closed around its rail.

“Move it.”

The man was still looking at Jinx.

“We were trying to help.”

She unfolded the scrap she had brought.

“You made me ugly.”

He looked at the painted face.

“It's a sign.”

“So am I, apparently.”

She held it beside her own face, then lowered it.

“Who told you to do this?”

“Nobody had to tell us. After what you did to the Council—”

Her mouth tightened.

“Finish that.”

He swallowed.

“They know we're not afraid anymore.”

Behind him, the porter tried to shift his wrists. Blood marked the cord where it cut into his skin.

Jinx looked at it.

Then at the medicine woman.

“You afraid?”

The woman nodded.

Jinx turned back.

“Seems like you missed one.”

Kaze laughed softly.

The man looked toward him, searching for an ally and finding an audience.

His right hand slipped beneath his coat.

Kaze's red eye followed the shoulder movement.

“Don't.”

The hand kept moving.

Black Marrow formed from Kaze's right palm, its narrow edge unfolding along the hand that held it. He stepped into the space beside the wagon and cut once.

The hand fell out of the coat without the rest of the arm.

A little mechanical striker remained clenched between its fingers.

Blood splashed across the grating.

The man screamed.

Behind us, the one holding Jesse at knife-point lunged.

The casing rang. His knife dropped through the grating and disappeared into the machinery below.


The railing shuddered beneath their weight.

At the wagon, another collector reached for a long gun hidden beneath a blanket.

Jinx's sidearm came up.

The discharge struck him in the chest. He slammed into the stacked crates and fell with the weapon still beneath the blanket.

One of the men at the upper stair fired toward her.

Arthur answered from the landing.

The man folded against the rail and slid down two steps.

The others scattered.

It had all gone wrong for them with remarkable efficiency. They had borrowed the reputations of two different monsters and put the collection point between them.

Sevika hauled the wagon sideways.

One wheel lifted, struck the bridge rail and stopped. She braced her metal arm against the frame and tore the rear board off.

Behind it lay a stoppered fuel jar, a packet of blasting powder and a crude ignition assembly with a wire running toward the severed hand.

The medicine woman backed down the steps.

Jinx looked into the wagon.

“That's what you were protecting people from?”

The collector clamped his remaining hand against the stump.

“Don't touch it.”

She leaned closer, examining the wire.

“You shouldn't have touched it either.”

* * *

Sevika kept the wagon steady while Jinx worked.

She removed the striker's wire from its terminal, took the ignition assembly out of the powder packet and set the two pieces well apart. Then she lifted the fuel jar and gave it to Jesse.

He stared at it.

“What am I doing with this?”

“Not dropping it.”

He put it beside the wall, gently enough to suggest he had developed a sudden religious conviction about glass.

Kaze watched the man bleed.

The severed hand twitched once around the striker.

He nudged it with the Black Marrow's tip.

“You would've blown your own fucking bridge.”

“They would've stopped.”

“Who?”

The man looked toward the upper city.

Jinx put the empty ignition housing on the wagon.

“No. You would've fallen off.”

Walter stepped around them to the bottles.

He checked the seals before asking anybody whether they were hurt.

Jesse saw him do it.

Then Jesse went to the porter and began untying the cord.

The dog released him reluctantly, lips lifting when the injured man tried to move his free hand toward his belt again.


This time, he did.

Sevika gathered the medicine crate and pushed it toward its owner.

“Take it.”

The woman took one step up, then stopped beside Jinx.

“Thank you.”

Jinx looked at her.

“I didn't send them.”

“I know.”

“Then don't tell people I did.”

The woman nodded.

It was not the answer Jinx wanted. Gratitude had already entered the room, and she could not find a clean place to put it down.

From above, someone began clapping.

Three sharp sounds.

Then silence.

The girl with the blue cloth had followed us to the upper landing. Her companions stood behind her. A porter from the waiting freight line leaned over the rail beside them.

“It is you,” the girl said.

Jinx looked up.

The girl's face had changed. I had seen that look outside shrines, before duels, and once on a man who had just discovered a horse he meant to steal was already saddled.

Possibility can look alarmingly similar in different people.

Jinx holstered her sidearm.

“Usually.”

“We knew you'd come.”

“Did you?”

“You broke their Council. They can't keep pretending we aren't here.”

Below the rail, the collector whimpered.

Jinx's gaze went to him.

Then back to the girl.

“You want that?”

The girl's eyes followed hers to the blood.

She hesitated.

“I want them to stop.”

For a moment, Jinx had no answer.

Then she picked up the forged passage notice and crumpled it.

“So do I.”

She meant the men on the bridge.

The girl heard something much larger.

* * *

The Black Marrow folded back through Kaze's right hand. He found a pouch of collected money beneath the wagon seat.

He weighed it in one hand.

Sevika watched him.

“That goes back.”

“To who?”

“The people they took it from.”

He looked toward the medicine woman, already walking down the alley with her crate.

Then at the other loads beneath the wagon.

“You keeping a list?”

Sevika indicated the folded papers stuffed beside the seat.

“Looks like they were.”

Kaze opened the pouch.

“Organized little fuckers.”

He took a handful of coins and put the pouch down.

Sevika caught his wrist.

The bridge seemed to get smaller around us.


Kaze looked at the metal fingers around his arm.

Then at Sevika.

She released him before he spoke.

“You can afford to leave that,” she said.

“I can afford a lot of things.”

“Then charge for something you're actually doing.”

He smiled.

“I did something.”

“You want payment, ask me.”

“You paying?”

“For a passage I can use.”

His attention shifted toward the upper road.

Sevika saw it and continued.

“Food. Medicine. People who need to get to work. I don't want to ask seven different men which lie they're selling every time something crosses.”

“Billy decides.”

“Then take me to Billy.”

Kaze put the coins back in the pouch.

He did it slowly, so she would understand it was a choice rather than a hand she had forced open.

“You can ask him yourself.”

Walter had stopped checking the bottles.

He looked toward Sevika with the stillness he used when another person stepped into a space he had already imagined owning.

Jesse was helping the porter lift the chemical crate onto the trolley.

“We got it,” he said. “Let's go.”

Walter did not move immediately.

Then he followed.

* * *

The gunshots reached Caitlyn while the warehouse owner was still trying to explain his signatures.

She left him with two officers and came onto the street.

Vi was already looking toward the service bridge.

A worker ran up the lower steps, stopped when he saw the uniforms and nearly turned back.

Caitlyn caught his attention.

“What happened?”

“The toll men. Somebody shot them.”

“Who?”

He pointed behind him.

“Her. Blue braids. The one on the notices.”

Vi's face changed.

“Jinx?”

The man nodded.

“And that black-arm fellow. Big dog. Others.”

Caitlyn called two officers from the warehouse entrance.

They went toward the bridge with her, keeping their weapons lowered while they passed the workers at the steps. Vi moved ahead, fast enough that Caitlyn had to lengthen her stride.

At the bend, another man was staggering uphill with blood running from his bitten arm.

He saw the uniforms and sat down hard against the wall.

“Get a physician,” Caitlyn told the officer beside her.

She kept moving.

Two others were dead: one against the crates, one on the upper stair.

Jinx was gone.

So were Kaze and the others.

The girl with the blue cloth remained above, watching the Enforcers arrive.

Caitlyn looked up at her.

“Which way?”

The girl said nothing.

Vi came to the foot of the stair.

“We need to find her.”

“Why?”

Vi looked at the dead man beside the rail.

Then back.

“Because people keep getting hurt.”

The girl indicated the wagon.

“They were hurting people before she came.”

“I can see that.”

“Can she?”

Her eyes moved toward Caitlyn.

Caitlyn heard the question. She stepped beside the bound porter's abandoned cord and picked up the forged notice Sevika had left on the wagon.

She read it.

Then looked at the girl again.

“Did Jinx tell them to do this?”

“No.”

“How do you know?”

“She said she didn't.”

“And you believe her?”

“She shot one of them.”

Vi closed her eyes for a moment.

Caitlyn put the notice into her file.

“I will record what happened here. It doesn't erase the Council attack.”

The girl looked down at her blue cloth.

“That didn't erase everything before it.”

There were replies Caitlyn could have made. Some would have been true. Some would have made the girl stop speaking.

She chose a smaller question.

“Which way did they go?”

The girl looked toward the upper turn.

It was not an answer she meant to give. Her eyes gave it anyway.

Vi saw.

* * *

Jinx reached Billy before the Enforcers reached the bridge.

We had come up the service stair, carrying no wounded and making no attempt to disguise ourselves. The steam and two sharp bends had hidden the upper road from the bridge long enough to get there.

Billy watched Jinx approach.

“Miss Jinx. You have acquired a queue.”

She looked back.

The medicine woman had taken the lower alley. But two freight porters, the girl's companions and a man with a covered basket were coming up behind Sevika, keeping far enough apart to pretend they had independent reasons.

“Not mine.”

“A popular claim tonight.”

Kaze set the warehouse cashbox beside the empty cart.

Billy's eyes followed it.

Sevika's did too.

“Where'd that come from?”

“Warehouse,” Kaze said.

She looked at the scorched paint on its side.

“Some of that's payroll.”

“Everybody keeps telling me.”

Her jaw tightened.

But she had come for something else, and she made herself turn toward Billy.

“I need passage for deliveries.”

“How delightful. Apparently I have opened a business.”

Walter stood behind her.

“An arrangement needs limits.”

Chris spoke from the banjo.

“You ain't the limit.”

Billy looked between them.

Then toward the people waiting below the closed shop.

“I shall decide who crosses. People may ask. They will not sell my permission on another street, and they will not announce themselves as my representatives.”

He smiled at Walter.

“Even with a very tidy trolley.”

Jesse let go of its handle.

Walter's voice became quieter.

“You are turning down something stable.”

“I am turning down you deciding for me.”

The banjo gave an agreeable little scrape.

Sevika indicated the waiting freight.

“A cart of food?”

“Bring it where I can see it.”

“Medicine?”

“The same.”

“A guard trying to take the driver?”

Billy considered her.

“Now that sounds entertaining.”

She had wanted a guarantee. She recognized what he had offered and did not mistake it for one.

Behind her, the porter with the covered basket moved forward.

He lifted the cloth.

Bread. Still warm enough for its smell to reach us.

Billy examined it with considerably more interest than he had given Walter's proposal.

“You may cross.”

The man stepped onto the firm strip beside the water.

Jinx watched him pass.

For a moment, she looked as if she might laugh.

Then she noticed a boyish-looking young man at the shop wall, copying the shape of her braids in blue chalk. He was grown, though his expression had the hopeful foolishness people are always trying to excuse as youth.

He had started with a torn warrant.

The printed face lay beside his knee. The drawn one smiled.

Jinx went over.

“I don't do that.”

He looked up.

“What?”

She indicated the smile.

He erased part of it with his sleeve.

“Better?”

“Worse.”

He stopped drawing.

Behind him, his companion had written OPEN beneath the face.

Jinx looked at the word.

“I didn't open it.”

“You kept the other men from closing it.”

“They were using my name.”

“We won't.”

She looked at the enormous face on the wall.

He followed her gaze and swallowed.

“Not like that.”

Daniel leaned over the cart.

“Your argument's got a picture in it.”

Jinx looked back at him.

“You helping?”

“Mostly watching.”

The young man touched the blue cloth tied around his own arm.

“We wanted them to know who we're with.”

“You're not with me.”

His hand fell.

She saw it.

For a few breaths, she stood between the painted face and the real people who had put it there.

Then she took the chalk from him.

She changed the eyes first. Cut the smile into something sharper. Added a jagged line beneath one braid.

“If you're going to make it ugly, do it properly.”

She gave the chalk back.

The young man stared at the wall.

His companion whispered, “Jinxers.”

Jinx heard.

She rolled her eyes and walked away.

Behind her, they began arguing about where to put the word.

* * *

Billy had let three more people cross when Jinx returned to him.

The watcher on the upper landing had left his notebook closed. He was following faces now, measuring them against something he had been told to remember.

Jinx looked toward the side stair.

Then at Billy.

“Can I see him?”

His expression changed, a little.

The banjo stayed quiet.

“You may.”

She nodded, but did not move closer yet.

“How's he been?”

Billy took a moment before answering.

“Alive. Restless. Himself in pieces.”

Her fingers tightened around the scrap of forged notice she still carried.

“Sounds familiar.”

Kaze stood from the cart.

At the lower stair, Vi appeared.

She stopped when she saw her sister.

Jinx saw her too.

The blue chalk dust on her hand became suddenly interesting. She wiped it against her trousers and left a mark.

Vi came up slowly.

“I heard about the bridge.”

“People talk fast.”

“Some of them.”

Vi looked at the new face on the shop wall. At the word beneath it. At Billy's water and the porter crossing with bread.

Then back at Jinx.

“Did you tell them to do that?”

“Which that?”

Vi let the question stand for a moment.

“Any of it.”

“No.”

It was almost true. There was chalk on her hand now.

Jinx looked behind Vi.

“Where's she?”

“On the bridge. With the injured.”

“And you're here.”

“Yes.”

Jinx's mouth moved as though there were several answers waiting behind it, none she wanted to give first.

Billy adjusted the banjo against his belly.

“I believe we have a visit to arrange.”

Vi looked up at him.

Then at her sister.

“Vander?”

Jinx nodded.

The sound below us changed as the bread cart reached the far landing. Its wheels knocked over the last joint in the paving, and the man pushing it called back that he was clear.

Billy let another person onto the strip.

Above the water, the two young painters finished adding their new word beneath Jinx's face. Neither had been asked to join an army. Neither had received an order.

They stood back to admire it anyway.

Jinx kept her eyes on Vi.

“You coming?”

Vi looked toward the stair she had climbed.

Then back.

“Yes.”

For once, Jinx did not mock the answer.

She stepped closer to Billy, still holding the crumpled paper that had tried to make her somebody else's commander.

Down on the bridge, Caitlyn's officers were writing her name into a new report.

Up here, somebody was writing it on a wall.

And between those two versions of her, the woman herself waited to see her father.

<!-- END EXACT SOURCE BYTES -->

## recovered-current-anchor-summary

This is a verbatim excerpt from the previously reconciled current-anchor summary, not recovered IX episode prose. Source: `KAZE_Ambessa_Arc_Amendment.md`; SHA256 of complete source: `31578c1c92d2143545b93a71b3b1254d4ecb333d4eec38ea9d361990b942eabd`.

- In repaired VIII.3, Rictus is already named at the ship and follows Ambessa during her encounter with the gang. Mel limits the escort to the vessel or accommodation and forbids independent patrols. Rictus needs expanded participation, not a replacement introduction.
- Ambessa has met Mel and the gang and has a strategic interest in Hextech. That encounter does not disclose their entire abilities or private plans to her.
- Cassandra's death and Caitlyn's subsequent command appointment remain established. Caitlyn commands the named Enforcer investigation; Ambessa's advisory role does not itself authorize independent Piltover arrests.
- X.1's checkpoint deaths, the Seven Sins' public naming, and X.2's warehouse theft and bridge violence remain actual events. Reports must reach Ambessa through identifiable people or records before she can use them.
- At X.2's endpoint, Vi and Jinx are waiting beside Billy to visit Vander. Caitlyn remains at the bridge. Ambessa and Rictus do not automatically know about that visit or Vander's location.

    "Briar": "Aboard Narrow Mercy sheltered side with retrieved same wood-backed portrait;14silver unchanged, pillory/notice/purse retained. Independent companion, no admission or romance development.",
    "Halven": "Original local former Briar handler/reclaiming participant in transport yard3streets below opening. Named by her before approach. Tried chain/cage recapture, ordered crossbow alive if possible, knife out/lunged; Briar killed by throat bite and fed. Corpse left below carriage step, knife not taken. Purse30silver and hamper stolen. No automatic named superior/global faction report.",
    "Halven_crossbowman": "Dead from Arthur one right UNSHAKEN chest shot after firing one bolt into Briar arm. Body/crossbow left by wagon wheel; no loot or revival.",
    "Halven_chain_escort": "Alive bleeding/breathing beneath broken packing table at group departure; struck by Briar pillory then Kaze wrist break/face slam. Sword never fully drawn; not taken. Briar began approach but stopped before feeding/attacking him. No later diagnosis/death/rescue assumed.",
    "transport_yard_workers": "Six ordinary laborers flee rear arch into dock lane. One shouts for help, another keeps running. No death/armed counterattack or completed authority notification shown.",
    "transport_yard": "Streetgate/rearloadingarch/crates/twoemptycages/coveredwagon/smallcarriage. Broken table/window and displaced crate, horses reared but not killed/freed/stolen. Two corpses/one wounded escort left; other freight untouched.",
    "loading_recess": "Temporary roofed loading recess/timber stack/rubbish barrel used for meal. Wooden stair to shuttered sailcloth loft, actual entry checked empty. No ownership/paid rental/new base. Spoiled pie stays barrel; all carried gear left with actors. Kaze/Briar used loft then left; no other items taken or new observed witness.",
    "blue_pump": "Agreed rendezvous one corner down from recess. Arthur/Daniel/Billy actually waited while Kaze/Briar upstairs; five now departed harborward. Bare ham bone in gutter, no personal gear left. No water use/cleanup performed merely because pump exists.",
    "portrait_artist": "New adultmale ordinary charcoalportraitseller at waterfrontstall, aliveuninjured. Acceptedactual1silverfromBriar.     "tattoo_artist": "Newordinarymale thirdshopartistbehind sailrepairer. Actualaffordable2silverplaininitialsafterotherquotes6and5; didTPPforDutch/Busta/BrianthenTPforCliffordwheninkranout. Offeredfinishfreefollowingday. Clifforddeliberatelyshotchest; fellagainst shelf,powerboxfell, stoppedbreathing, definitivelydead. Body/cup/tattoogun/wires/boxstay shop. Two paidcoins takenbackbyBusta; no otherloot/newpremisedvillainidentity.",
    "sail_repairer": "Livingordinaryneighborheardgunshot, stoppedhammering, approachedshopwithhammer. SawBustaandDutchrisingbesidebody; backedtoowndoorunderverbalintimidation. TPPpassedanddeparted, thenheactuallyshoutedforwatch. No death, arrest, chase, dispatchednamedauthority or townwideknowledgeestablished.",
    "Narrow_Mercy": "Beyond harbor mouth after temporary mooring at maintenance landing and actual cast-off. Southward coastal voyage begins; original crew/fourfitters/pump freight retained and protected, not seized by Sins. No Zaun arrival.",
    "Harl": "Commands own departing ship; allowed brief commercial pause with temporary shore line then resumed under improved wind. No crew/ship struck during shore fight; captain terms retained.",
    "Pell": "Aboard, explaining actual hired pump connection to Chris through Billy view/banjo. Reports recruiter described replacement pumps for a stopping line, openly has never seen plant; not recruited into Sins.",
    "departed_fitters": "Older grey-bearded fitter and one younger fitter voluntarily left before sailing; exact onward destinations unknown. Original six now four aboard.",
    "supplier_agent_and_guards": "Agent in red waistcoat left pier with both guards and his own contract copy. Bloody nose, one guard cradles wrist, all alive. Threatens to tell Heisenberg; actual transmission/receipt not shown.",
    "crane_operator": "Confirmed dead after Kaze two mast impacts, body on quay where dropped, not taken into BELLY or harvested. No identity/faction beyond ordinary operator established.",
    "loading_pier": "Severed empty-hook chain; hook embedded in paving beside intact gangplank route, blood/bone on central mast, torn cab rail on pier. Twin-arm crane damaged, not entire port destroyed. Witnesses heard crash/some running; no watch arrival or global report.",
    "Heisenberg": "Offpage retained Season 2 dominant Zaun operation and first Bilgewater foothold. Contract/captain substantiate named Rivermark receiving works; exact plant interiors, stock, defenses and report propagation unknown. Has not yet learned E18 dock events at episode close.",
    "Deadpool": "Alive at hired port loading stage with Hadran/clerk,40silver total fee now received, two purchased local swords recovered, torn red suit/right sleeve cut away and Chris cloth. Painful injuries materially healing; functional right-hand grip returns by close, no complete all-injury/reset assertion. Independent, not Sin or permanent BELLY resident.",
    "bronze_horse": "Actual E14 sculpture physically released by Billy into padded broad hired lighter, inspected and bought back for120silver. Reached hired loading stage; being brought ashore by Hadran people. Green bridle stones/gilding intact. No longer Sins inventory.",
    "cargo_lighter_and_boatmen": "Original local hired broad cargo lighter with two boatmen, loading pads/ropes, reached hired stage with horse/clerk/Wade. Hadran funded hire, exact fee unquantified. Boat/crew unharmed.",
    "breakwater_landing": "Two corner blocks broken off by Kaze slamming Wade, fallen into water; blood and new gap persist. Separate from E18 damaged crane/operator corpse. No entire port closure or remote institutional response granted."
  },

  "actor_knowledge_changes": [
    "genuine threat sense prompts evasive turn when assault starts, not advance omniscience.",
    "This is his false attribution/control motive, not objective proof Kaze commands membership. No outside employer, secret old deal, coercion or possession invented.",
    "Canine perception/choice described; no translated political reason or universal human moral understanding. Kaze treats turning against them as betrayal.",
    "Survivors present perceive actual conflict/deaths to their real sight/hearing; Chris through Billy feed/outward banjo only, not an unrestricted outside angle. Arthur hears objection and challenges choice. Frank actually checks corpses.",
    "Billy states Jack is preserved and he controls potential burial discussion; no corpse released to Dutch or new responsibility adjudicated. Dutch offpage unaware of E7; no paid betrayal/automatic TPP recruitment.",
    "Busta/Brian at their last market departure, Sella/cooper at wages custody, remote city/imperial actors get no automatic report. Port bustle/dust/sound does not establish named witnesses or identity knowledge.",
