Season 3, Episode 19: A Modest Recovery Fee

The Sins were leaving a Noxian port aboard the Narrow Mercy, following a delivery to a drug lord they meant to rob. They had almost reached the end of the harbor when a man in a red mask stood up in the boat pursuing them.

“Excellent news! You're being approached by a professional.”

The clerk beside him grabbed his belt and pulled him down before an oar caught his knee.

“Sit.”

“That's the customer-service department. Terrible reviews.”

Two boatmen were rowing a broad, empty cargo lighter toward the ship's stern. The clerk pointed past its rail at Billy, the enormous catfish standing beside Arthur.

“That's him. That's the creature that swallowed it.”

The masked man studied Billy.

Then his voice slipped somewhere it had no business being.

Hi. Wade Wilson. Deadpool, if you've already paid for the merchandise. I've been in this medieval tetanus aquarium for two nights, and today I became a licensed recovery specialist. There is no license. I asked. Apparently you just need a sword and the confidence of a man selling his own sperm out of a cooler.

“What the fuck? Who's this nigga taking my narration job?”

That was me, by the way. Mimir. The fellow who'd been doing it perfectly well before the walking groin rash arrived.

Wade tilted his head.

Ooh. Angry Scottish audiobook. Does he come with the boat?

The clerk shoved a small purse into his chest.

“Ten now. Thirty when the horse is back at the house. Those were the terms.”

Wade caught the purse.

“And I'm delighted to return to our regular programming.”

Aye. You fucking are.

The clerk had worked at Master Hadran's private sale that afternoon. He'd watched the Sins rob the buyers, watched Arthur return from killing Samira, and watched Billy swallow a bronze horse from the courtyard. Finding a ten-foot catfish along the waterfront had proved easier than finding the courage to approach him again.

He'd brought help this time. Hadran's money had bought Wade two local swords, neither quite the length he preferred, and hired this lighter to carry the sculpture home. The clerk kept a second, larger purse beneath his coat.

Wade tucked away his advance and raised both empty hands.

“We'd like to buy your horse.”

Arthur came to the rail.

“Ain't got a horse out here.”

“Yes, and I'd have opened with that, but my associate has been very specific about the fish.”

Billy leaned over him.

“Have we met?”

The clerk's mouth tightened.

“You swallowed the chest I filled.”

“Then we have.”

Harl, the captain, came down from the raised stern. He looked at the lighter, then at Arthur.

“More of your friends?”

“Possible buyer.”

“That wasn't a reassuring distinction.”

Arthur nodded toward the cargo boat. “He gets his goods, we get paid. Don't need to come aboard.”

Harl considered the water beyond the harbor wall. The wind had barely begun filling his sail. He ordered it eased, letting the ship lose way beside a maintenance landing. A crewman passed a temporary line around its mooring ring while the boatmen drew alongside.

“Do your business. When that wind settles, we're going.”

The lighter bumped softly against fenders hanging over the hull. One boatman looped a line around a low cleat, beside the ship's short rope ladder. Beyond them, a flight of narrow maintenance steps descended from the stone breakwater into the water.

Kaze looked down at Wade's crossed sword grips.

“You buying something or cutting it?”

“I'm versatile.”

“You look like you work at a strip club for burns victims.”

“And you look like the tattoo artist ran out of questions.”

Kaze grinned, gold above silver.

Wade pointed at the lettering across his stomach.

“Convenient name tag, though. Mine's on the underwear. Makes introductions a whole event.”

“I like him,” Daniel said.

He stood beside Arthur with his spear against the rail and his bottle in hand. His lip had closed. The shoulder he'd tested while saving the pump moved easily now, though the rest of him remained enthusiastically drunk.

“Give him a minute,” Arthur said.

Inside Billy, Chris leaned closer to the live view on his second monitor. His repaired mask rested nearby, the patched cowl folded beside it. He'd set the working headlamp down when the shouting started.

“Ask him what he's offering,” he said through Billy's banjo. “I'm not putting that horse back in a fucking courtyard for compliments.”

Wade looked at the instrument.

“Was that the fish?”

“No,” Billy said.

“Great. One mystery at a time.”

The clerk stood carefully.

“Master Hadran will pay forty silver for its return. Assuming it's undamaged.”

Arthur stared at him.

“Hundred and twenty.”

The clerk sat down again.

“You cannot ask him to buy his own property.”

“Already did.”

Wade turned toward the clerk.

“He's got us there. The asking happened. We all heard it.”

“Then recover it.”

The clerk spoke quietly, but the anger had carried farther than he meant. He glanced at Billy, then back at Wade.

“That's why you're here.”

Wade's manner changed a little. Still loose, still ready with something unpleasant to say, but his hands had stopped wandering.

“We haven't tried sixty.”

“I am not paying these people.”

Arthur folded his arms.

“You brought a buyer's purse and a feller with swords. Sounds like you weren't certain yourself.”

The clerk looked at the gun beneath Arthur's coat.

“There was a woman at that sale. Samira. She tried to stop you.”

“I remember.”

“They carried her off the terrace after you left.”

Arthur's expression didn't change.

Wade looked between them. The clerk had described armed thieves. Hearing how the afternoon had ended gave the description a weight it hadn't had when the swords were being bought.

“And we're getting the horse,” Wade said quietly to him. “That's the job.”

The clerk swallowed.

“Yes.”

Wade nodded. He hadn't been hired to avenge a stranger. He also wasn't volunteering the boatmen for whatever happened when he tried to take something from these people.

Wade looked up at Kaze.

“Can we borrow those steps? I'd rather have this conversation away from the two gentlemen currently regretting their hourly rate.”

The boatmen said nothing. One had already moved his hand off the cleat.

Kaze glanced at the stone steps, then at the narrow landing above them.

“You think beating me gets you the horse?”

“I think asking nicely is having a difficult quarter.”

“You haven't asked nicely.”

Wade took a breath.

“Please return the stolen horse.”

“No.”

“There. Now we have a process.”

Kaze laughed and stepped onto the rail.

Arthur caught his eye.

“We still want paying.”

“I heard you.”

Wade climbed onto the lower steps while Kaze jumped past him, landing on the stone above. The distance was short enough that the clerk could still hear them over the water. Harl folded his hands behind his back and waited with the expression of a man reviewing several recent decisions.

Briar stayed on the ship. She'd tucked her wood-backed portrait beneath a coil of clean sailcloth, out of the spray, and stood where she could see both men.

“Does the mask help?” she asked Wade.

He looked back.

“Immensely. You should see the alternative.”

“I mean with fighting.”

“Oh. No. Mostly the other thing.”

Wade drew the longer sword with his right hand. The shorter stayed sheathed across his back. He climbed the final step and kept the wall at his left, giving himself room to retreat along the narrow landing.

Kaze had no weapon in his hands.

“You kill people for forty silver?” he asked.

“I recover garden ornaments. You're making this political.”

Wade lunged.

Kaze's red eye followed the shoulder before the blade moved. He turned just enough to let the thrust pass his ribs, caught Wade's wrist in his left hand, and squeezed.

Wade went with the pressure instead of resisting it. He stepped in, drove his forehead into Kaze's face and drew the short blade left-handed. Its edge opened Kaze's bare side.

Black blood ran over the tattooed skin.

Kaze looked down at it. He hadn't tried to stop the cut.

Wade tried to pull his right hand free.

The bones in his wrist broke first.

“Oh, you absolute—”

Kaze drove a knee into his stomach. Air left Wade in a strangled cough. The long sword fell from his useless hand and clattered against the wall.

Wade slashed upward with the short one.

Black Marrow grew into Kaze's right hand through the draw across his left hip. The living sword met the rising edge, caught it close to the hilt, and turned it away from his throat. Kaze released the broken wrist, stepped through the opening and struck Wade's chest with his left palm.

Wade flew backward along the landing. His shoulder hit the wall hard enough to crack the corner stone. He dropped the short sword, bounced onto his knees and tried to breathe through a chest that had briefly forgotten its shape.

Kaze walked toward him.

The cut in Kaze's side was already drawing closed. The blood on his skin stayed where it was.

Wade saw it.

“Oh. You have the subscription too.”

He snatched up a loose stone with his good hand and threw it at Kaze's face. Kaze knocked it aside. It struck the wall behind Wade and fell beside the short sword.

Wade rolled toward the fallen short sword.

Kaze's left eye changed focus.

For an instant, a stone occupied the place where Kaze had been. Kaze himself stood where the stone had lain, only a few paces away, directly in Wade's path.

He'd exchanged positions with something real and close enough to reach with that particular eye. Wade got no chance to mistake it for running.

“Well, that feels like a different subscription.”

Kaze caught him by the front of his suit before he could stand. The first slam drove his back into the cracked corner. The second broke it.

Two blocks toppled into the water. Wade went down on the remaining stone, mouth wet behind his mask, a jagged piece of his forearm bone pushing through the red sleeve where the broken wrist had folded beneath him.

Kaze planted a black shoe on his chest.

The landing fell quiet except for the wash below and Wade trying to draw breath.

“You still recovering that horse?” Kaze asked.

Wade lifted his good hand a little.

“Exploring alternative methods.”

The bone at his wrist moved. Slowly, horribly, it began drawing back beneath the torn sleeve.

Kaze watched with sudden interest.

Wade wasn't unhurt. The smallest shift made his breath catch. But the injury was changing beneath the blood, and his chest was beginning to rise more evenly.

“How much of you can I take off?” Kaze asked.

Wade looked up at him.

“That's usually a third-date question.”

Kaze put more weight on his chest.

“I asked you something.”

The joke left Wade's voice.

“Enough that I won't be getting home soon.”

“You got a home?”

“Yeah.”

Kaze waited.

“People in it,” Wade added. “I just got them back.”

From the ship, Arthur called, “Kaze.”

Kaze kept looking at the man beneath his shoe.

“He paying a hundred and twenty yet?”

“About to find out.”

Kaze took his foot away.

Wade stayed down for another breath before rolling onto his unbroken side. Whatever he'd expected from this job, he'd adjusted his expectations considerably.

Kaze dismissed his sword and picked up Wade's two blades. He carried them down the steps, dropped them into the lighter and pointed at the clerk.

“Don't lend him those again.”

The clerk looked at the blood above the steps.

“He attacked you.”

“Yeah. I was there.”

Wade came down sideways, using the wall. His right hand hung badly, but it was hanging in the correct direction again.

The clerk leaned toward him.

“You said you could handle them.”

“I did handle him. Briefly. He handled me with greater commitment.”

Arthur looked down from the rail.

“Hundred and twenty. Horse goes home. You go home. Everybody gets what they came for.”

“I came for the horse without paying a hundred and twenty.”

“Well, now you've seen both prices.”

The clerk opened his coat. He untied the larger purse, counted its contents into his lap and stopped with his thumb resting on the final little stack.

There was enough. Hadran had given him room to bargain. The clerk had hoped not to spend it.

“I want to see it first.”

Billy approached the side of the ship.

“You may wish to move your feet.”

The boatmen shifted the lighter around to give him its open middle. They laid two old loading pads across the bottom. Billy's mouth widened. The gilded muzzle of the bronze horse appeared between his teeth, followed by its mane and raised foreleg.

He lowered it gradually, guiding its weight down with his hands. The lighter settled deeper in the water. Its broad bottom took the sculpture without putting a boatman beneath it.

The clerk leaned forward. He checked the green stones in the bridle, then the gilded mane and the raised foreleg.

Wade looked at the golden detail farther back.

“Somebody loved this horse very specifically.”

“It is the right one,” the clerk said.

“I wasn't worried about a second pair.”

The clerk paid Arthur. Billy waited until the coins were aboard before releasing his final hold on the sculpture.

Kaze climbed back onto the Narrow Mercy. Behind him, Wade tried to pull the purse containing his advance from his pocket. His injured fingers closed, failed, and made him hiss through his teeth.

Chris had watched the whole exchange through the live feed.

“Bring him up here a second.”

Arthur glanced at Billy.

“Why?”

“His sleeve's caught in the wound. Got clean cloth in here. Besides, I want to ask him something.”

Wade looked up.

“Does the banjo have medical insurance?”

“I have scissors and a bad attitude.”

Billy considered Wade, then spoke to him directly.

“Leave your swords with your associate. You may visit. You may also decline.”

“Visit what?”

Billy smiled.

Wade looked from the mouth to the horse.

“Right. We have covered the entrance.”

The clerk objected immediately.

“We have to get back.”

“You have your horse. Give me a minute to stop bleeding on it.”

Wade left the blades where Kaze had dropped them, climbed the rope ladder with his good arm doing most of the work, and agreed to Billy's offer.

Billy swallowed him.

Inside, Wade arrived on the raised living platform and stood very still.

There was a workshop. A lounge. Ordinary furniture on a floor that was emphatically not ordinary. A man at a workbench was waving him over. Nearby, a small, round piece of meat passed a camera to another man and came closer.

“You look like you got hurt,” the meat said.

Wade looked down.

“And you look like lunch got a scholarship.”

“I'm Meatwad.”

“Of course you are.”

Charles held the camera steady.

“What brings you into our community?”

“Workplace injury.”

“And what do you hope to find here?”

“An adult who owns these scissors.”

Chris took Wade's forearm gently enough to examine it, then cut the torn fabric away from the closing wound. He handed him the clean cloth.

“Press that where it still bleeds. I'm Chris.”

“Wade.”

“That hurt as bad as it looked?”

“Worse. He did it in front of the client.”

Chris laughed.

“Had a chef throw a ladle at me once in front of an entire wedding party. Bride caught it. Best hands in the building. Married a feller who couldn't hold a job.”

Wade looked around the workshop again.

“You live in here?”

“Yeah.”

“Willingly?”

“You met the people outside.”

Charles stepped closer.

“We try to provide an environment where people can discover—”

“He means Billy,” Chris said. “Billy provides it. Charles discovered my couch.”

Wade turned toward the camera.

“Charles what?”

“Manson.”

Wade's masked face stayed pointed at him.

Then he looked back at Chris.

“You buried the lead under the talking hamburger.”

Meatwad sounded concerned. “I ain't a hamburger.”

“You're right. That was reductive.”

“I don't have bread.”

Charles handed the camera to Meatwad and pulled the interview chair closer, placing himself beside Wade.

“We're making a record of what really happens here.”

“Manson and Meatwad's Inside Job,” Meatwad said proudly.

Wade looked between them.

“That is a spectacularly incriminating title.”

Chris began putting his scissors away.

“Only happens in here. Camera stays inside. You don't want filming, tell them.”

Wade considered the lens.

“All right. Keep rolling.”

He sat down, straightened his good shoulder and held the cloth against his injured arm below the edge of the picture.

“My name is Wade Wilson. Today I successfully recovered a valuable sculpture through a combination of negotiation, physical courage and rapid skeletal restructuring.”

Chris leaned against the bench.

“He broke your fucking arm.”

“And yet the horse is going home. Results, Christopher.”

Charles nodded gravely, as though this vindicated a belief he had personally developed.

“People don't understand the sacrifices of leadership.”

“Were you there?” Wade asked.

“I'm speaking generally.”

“Then generally move your chair. You're blocking my good side.”

Meatwad shifted the camera, trying to include both of them. Charles shifted with it.

Wade watched him do it a second time.

“Holy shit. You're like a haunted weather vane.”

Chris laughed hard enough to look away.

For a moment Wade seemed pleased simply to have landed one. Then Meatwad asked where he'd come from, and the answer arrived without the performance.

“Home. Went out with my gear. Came through a door and found a street that definitely wasn't mine. Went back through it. Different room.”

“You try the door again?”

“A lot.”

Meatwad lowered the camera a little.

“Maybe it was the wrong door.”

Wade looked at him.

“Yeah. Maybe.”

“Who's there?” Meatwad asked.

Wade looked at the camera, then at the little creature holding it.

“People who kept a seat for me at dinner. I spent a long time worrying I wouldn't have that again. Finally got a whole table, and then…”

He opened his good hand toward the room around him.

Charles began to lean forward. Chris caught his eye and gave one small shake of his head.

Meatwad waited.

“I don't know what they're being told,” Wade said. “That's the part I don't like.”

“You can sit here till you find out.”

Wade was quiet for a moment.

“Thanks, buddy.”

The offer hadn't provided a road home. It had, however, been an offer. He seemed to know the difference and appreciate it anyway.

Meatwad glanced toward Chris.

“We got another chair?”

“We can find one.”

Charles shifted in his own seat, evidently relieved that the proposal hadn't begun with an inventory of occupied furniture.

Chris gave him space to sit with that before asking, “You got anything that opens another one?”

“If I did, I'd be home watching somebody else have a terrible day.”

He lifted the cloth. The wound had closed, leaving blood and ragged fabric around skin that was knitting unevenly. He tried his fingers again. This time they curled far enough to hold it.

“Okay. Good enough to invoice.”

Charles leaned toward him.

“You could stay for another interview.”

Wade stood.

“Charles, you've achieved something extraordinary. You've made the giant fish's mouth seem like the sensible exit.”

Chris asked Billy to let him out. The reply came through the live exterior feed, and the route opened at Billy's decision.

Wade emerged onto the ship beside the rail. The clerk was still below with the horse. The boatmen had secured it with their loading ropes and were becoming steadily less interested in the conversations of rich men.

Wade climbed down carefully, collected his swords and wiped the remaining blood from his hand with Chris's cloth.

Kaze watched him test his grip.

“You coming back?”

“Depends. Are you stealing anything else from my employer?”

Arthur looked at the clerk.

“We done?”

The clerk tied the empty negotiation purse shut.

“For today.”

Wade looked toward Kaze.

“There's your answer. Deeply reassuring.”

The boatman released the line. The lighter pulled away, carrying the horse, the clerk and a recovery specialist whose right sleeve would never again support his version of events.

Harl had the temporary shore line cast off and ordered the sail trimmed. This time the canvas filled firmly.

“How long to Zaun?” Arthur asked.

“Three weeks if the weather behaves. Longer if it doesn't. South along the coast first. Water and stores at the stops I choose. Then the Piltover approach and the receiving berth.”

Arthur nodded. Following an actual delivery would put them somewhere useful at the far end. For now, the captain's route suited him.

Pell, one of the four fitters traveling with the machinery, had heard them from beside the secured pump.

“You know those are replacements?” he asked.

Chris answered through the banjo. “The pumps?”

Pell nodded, then remembered whom he was talking to.

“Yes. Recruiter said a production line kept stopping. We're being sent to get it running properly. Maybe they've sorted it by the time we arrive. I haven't seen the place.”

Chris considered that.

“I figured he was expanding.”

“Might be doing that too. Wasn't what they hired me for.”

Arthur came over to the pump. Its frame was still marked where Kaze and Daniel had kept it from crushing the rail.

“So White's waiting on this.”

“Somebody there is,” Pell said. “That's what I actually know.”

Chris asked him which piece he'd been hired to fit. Pell pointed it out to Billy's view and explained the connection without pretending he'd designed the whole plant. Chris listened. His next question was better than his first.

Arthur left them to it. A working business could be worth taking from. A business desperate to keep working might offer a different opportunity. He wanted to see which one waited at the end of Harl's route before choosing where to put his hands.

He counted out the horse money while the harbor wall slid past. Seventy-two silver for his share of the earlier robbery. Twelve apiece for Kaze, Daniel, Chris and Billy. Briar hadn't taken part in that score; the agreement about her participation concerned the job ahead.

Daniel put his twelve away, bringing his purse to seventy, and took a drink.

“Still think I could've sold it for more.”

Arthur glanced at him.

“You were gonna advertise that it didn't eat.”

“And look who bought it. Man already knew.”

Billy received Chris's coins for transfer inside, kept his own share, and looked back toward the diminishing lighter.

“A rather agreeable fellow once somebody interrupted him.”

“Which somebody?” Arthur asked.

Billy smiled without answering.

Briar retrieved her portrait from the sailcloth and moved to the sheltered side of the deck. Kaze joined the others at the rail. Behind them, the damaged crane and the gap in the breakwater grew smaller. Neither had repaired itself because its authors were leaving.

By the time the lighter reached its hired loading stage, the Narrow Mercy was beyond the harbor mouth.

Hadran had come down to meet the returning boat. His satisfaction lasted until the clerk told him the price.

“You paid them?”

“You authorized the purse.”

“In the event that you couldn't recover it.”

The clerk glanced at Wade's sleeve.

Hadran followed the look.

“The agreement was delivery to the house,” he said.

The clerk pointed to the waiting handcart and the two porters Hadran had brought down with him.

“Then they can finish it. Unless you'd like to send him back for the money.”

Wade stood very still while his client considered that suggestion. Nobody reached for the oars.

Wade held out his newly functioning hand.

“Thirty remaining.”

Hadran stared at him, then at the sculpture being brought ashore. The green stones were intact. So was the gilding. He counted the coins himself and dropped them into Wade's palm.

“I won't be employing you again.”

Wade closed his hand around the money.

“That's all right. Your horse keeps getting involved with a rough crowd.”
